## Introduction

In much the same way there used to be time in my life to write and record songs, and install and customize a Linux installation from the command line, I used to have a lot more time to roll up RPG characters. 

And so you might ask, how on earth would I have time to create an entire book of them? A fair question, which I will consider as I first attempt to complete this introduction. 

This is not a tome of random, disconnected individuals. To me, that would be fantasy on a level beyond the game this book is written for. While there are some high-functioning outliers and fringe individuals, there are also many friendships, families, organizations, rivalries, and other interpersonal dynamics within the book. Some members are famous to the general public; others only well-known within their circles. So, although this is a book of NPCs, many of them aren't exactly strangers. 

For simplicity’s sake, this collection is limited to the four basic races and classes specified in the Basic Fantasy RPG Core Rules. 

Also, I’m somewhat of a Grognard purist who believes that the steady churn creating more and more distinct classes is more than just a cashgrab. It’s a symptom of treating role-playing gamers in much the way that American television sitcoms treat their audiences—like idiots that enjoy flavour, but need it spoon-fed. But if you’re reading this, chances are you deeply appreciate the types of shows that don’t pander to you, but rather trust in and play to your intelligence. If you’re a GM, you also understand how hastily constructed but poorly playtested classes can break the game. 

When I first learned tabletop role-play, it was an exercise in creativity, problem solving, and improvisation. A player action was often whatever you decided to try to attempt. A ranger was just a fighter with a demonstrated interest in tracking, horseback riding, and favouring longbow attacks at distance before dismounting to engage with a longsword. Similarly, an assassin was a particularly vile thief that eerily enjoyed taking your money and your life—ofering it as a service-for-hire when they weren’t otherwise busy plundering dungeons. 

If the Game Master asked why a particular action was plausible, you gave your best explanation based on who you believed your character was and what they could do. A good GM would answer with a yes or no. A great GM would roll behind the screen and tell you whether it worked or not. Not knowing what might work kept the creativity flowing around the table. Not rigidly defining what characters should be like, left a lot of blanks that players filled in themselves. And for the GM, ruling on creative solutions on a case-by-case basis allowed PCs to evolve naturally over time in a way that didn’t become overpowered. 

To the greatest extent possible, I implore you to take the members of these four standard classes and further distinguish them as fully-realized characters through enhanced personalities, preferences, hopes, flaws, quirks, and actions. Don’t think of them as a rigid class member, but as people with a set of established skills and talents—but eager to grow beyond them. Consider everything in this book as a starting default: names, genders, stats, backgrounds. Look for their connections and memberships and add and subtract from them. Create new organizations and factions, and specify the motivations and plans for existing groups and individuals that have been left intentionally vague or undefined. Integrate these people into your world, or build a world on top of them. 

As a pre-teen GM, this is the kind of book I always wished had existed. I hope you find it useful. 

If there are more pages after this one — pages with characters, even — hooray! I have no explanation. Sorry. But do enjoy. 

## First-Level Characters

### Clerics (1d8)

### 1. Idril Lorwynn

A beginner in every sense, Idril has not learned to trust her instincts. She has the requisite mind and the discipline to attain a brilliant and successful career, but none of the confidence. She’s still amazed that she’s achieved what little she has and she requires constant positive feedback. It’s all a bit too taxing for would-be friends and mentors alike. Whatever positive impression she makes upon first meeting is ultimately undone by her near-constant neediness. 

Elf Cleric 1, AC 14, AB +1, #At 1 mace, Dam 1d8, Mv 30', ML 9 

STR 11, INT 15 (+1), WIS 17 (+2), 

DEX 9, CON 12, CHA 7 (-1) 

Equipment: leather armour, shield, mace 

HP 5 ☐☐☐☐☐ 

### 2. Allura Crosse

Her parents had hoped that their choice of a name would be prophetic, and indeed she grew into a woman of rarely paralleled beauty. It was to their great disappointment that she chose a life of expedition and service over children, hearth, and home. She has no regrets and a wellpractised retort for any who dare to disagree with her life choices. 

Human Cleric 1, AC 14, AB +1, #At 1 Mace +1, 

Dam 1d8+1, Mv 30', ML 9 

STR 12, INT 8 (-1), WIS 16 (+2), 

DEX 10, CON 11, CHA 18 (+3) 

Equipment: leather armour, shield, Mace +1 HP 6 ☐☐☐☐☐ ☐ 

### 3. Ash Bronsone

As the now-adult child of a grave digger, Ash is quite content to have made it to the clergy. It was those gleaming white robes, mastery of language and command of peoples’ attention which captivated that young mind so many years ago. Though now respected and successful, Ash remains ever-aware of how it was to be poor, and carries out the role of Cleric of the Temple of New Birth with purest intent. The dress code of the high priests and priestesses is somewhat concerning. It seems that gleaming white robes are not in the future—not at this temple at least.

### 4. Elton Hillame

Uncommonly proud of his family’s long history as the founders and builders of the region, Elton is easily distracted from his quest and his faith to weave long tales about his ancestors’ stellar achievements. He even has scrolls tucked away with extensive charts of over 15 generations of his lineage, including his own handwritten notes in the margins. Ask about them—at your peril.

DEX 15 (+1) CON 17 (+2), CHA 11

HP 8 ☐☐☐☐☐ ☐☐☐ 

### 5. Becket Roundtree

When not enjoying his own company, Becket prefers the companionship of plants. He’s learning to cope with the increasing necessity to interact with clergy colleagues and flock, but in his estimation the Creator lives in all creation. That is where he longs to be, and given suficient opportunity he will use his concealment abilities to savour a quick recharge—up in a tree or nestled in tall grass. He’s always there for you in the end, but you’re third choice. It’s not your fault. Probably.

### 6. Gisberta Bognárh

Eternally optimistic, Gisberta chooses to see only the good in others. It’s also been suggested that she’s incapable of doing otherwise. While wise within her sphere of knowledge, she isn’t gifted at reading people or their intentions, nor does she easily remember a face. Many thieves seeking support from the (now poorly-named) Temple of Plenty learned to visit her weekly, making minor alterations to their disguises. She was only too happy to give. And give.

### 7. Arna Bloms

The way to Arna’s heart is through trinkets and knickknacks. She’s collected hundreds in her lifetime. Each is a reminder of an experience or a friendship, and she is wont to travel with up to a dozen at a time. A previous adventuring party learned by way of a high-speed wagon collision that these souvenirs can function as deadly projectiles. Should your sling run out of stones, her “ammunition” is usually nearby. She’ll forgive you. Just buy (or make) her something else.

### 8. Wilhelmine Bösch

From her earliest days, it was clear that Wilhelmine felt a deep sense of responsibility for everyone in her circle. She was born to parents late in their lives and tended to their health while still a child. She rescued animals in the neighbourhood and brought food and clothing to people in need in her community. As a Cleric, she worked out of the Temple of Want for only a short time because it was clear that many in the community—and the region—needed help but could not make their way to her to get it. So, she set of and has yet to settle anywhere. Now it seems that only the world will be enough for Wilhelmine.

### Fighters (1d8)

### 1. Tasia Lentinus

Tasia (pronounced TAH-zha, if you please) is the name of a woman who grew from a girl named Anastasia. That girl wore dresses under duress, took dance lessons under supervision, and learned manners under the yoke of a yew switch. It was never the person she was meant to be. Tasia now dresses in chain mail, cleaves heads and limbs with the grace of a ballerina, and obeys none but her own conscience. 

Human Fighter 1, AC 16, AB +1, #At 1 battle axe, 

Dam 1d8+1, Mv 20', ML 9 

STR 13 (+1), INT 14 (+1), WIS 10, 

DEX 11, CON 12, CHA 14 (+1) 

Equipment: chain mail, shield, battle axe

### 2. Beathan O'Brien

Most folks around town are struggling to fill their cups each day. For a few, their cup runneth over. Beathan insists that you continue to fill his cup while he slurps the over-spill from the tabletop. He fights harder, sings louder, and eats and drinks more than anyone he knows. All these have increased steadily as he’s travelled more of the world and met others like himself. While everyone hopes he’s now reached the peak of his excess, who knows? It’s a shame, really; he’s not an idiot or a brute. He has alarming depth which might result in actual leadership abilities someday, if he can just outlive his current habits. 

Human Fighter 1, AC 17, AB +1, #At 1 shortsword, Dam 1d6+2, Mv 20', ML 9 STR 16 (+2), INT 14 (+1), WIS 16 (+2), DEX 14 (+1), CON 12, CHA 11 Equipment: chain mail, shield, shortsword HP 5 ☐☐☐☐☐ 

### 3. Magnhild the Mighty

Awkward when asked about his nickname, Magnhild will insist it’s just something he’s always been called. In truth, it’s a nickname he invented for himself to conceal his lack of a surname. As a child dumped anonymously at the orphanage, he was never told it—so it’s long since lost, along with any living relatives. He was a labourer, then briefly worked as an on-call substitute in the fighting pits, but learned he didn’t have the heart for it. He saw other men like himself, desperate and lost, fighting someone they had no quarrel with to put bread on the table. What he really wants are people to fight alongside with, and a purpose.

### 4. Beornræd Fairchilde

Second son of a wainwright, his first armour was a wagon-wheel tower shield and a pole arm fashioned from a broken falling tongue. Like his brother, he learned by watching others in combat then practising in the field after chores. With no formal instruction in adulthood, he remains to this day a keen student. Observation, replication, observation, alteration, repeat. Never boastful about his abilities and never hesitant to share what he’s learned with others, he’s about the best friend and party member one could wish for. 

Halfling Fighter 1, AC 15, AB +1, #At 1 battle axe, Dam 1d8+1, Mv 20', ML 9 STR 15 (+1), INT 12, WIS 13 (+1), DEX 11, CON 14 (+1), CHA 13 (+1) Equipment: chain mail, battle axe (two-handed) HP 5 ☐☐☐☐☐ 

### 5. Alke Fairburne

When invaders poured into her village, Alke discovered her talent for strategy and leadership. Securing the cooperation of women and children around her, she used wagons and carts and debris to corral the horde into the square while the men engaged them hand to hand. Penned by barriers, the villagers rained fire, stones, and arrows on the invaders from above. While losses of life and property were substantial, the enemy was destroyed. In the aftermath, every home was given a weapon to hang on the wall, and every resident instructed on how to react more quickly in the future. Alke founded a militia and trained her replacement before setting of alone. 

Human Fighter 1, AC 17, AB +1, #At 1 spear, Dam 1d8, Mv 20', ML 9 STR 12, INT 15 (+1), WIS 8 (-1), DEX 16 (+2), CON 14 (+1), CHA 12 Equipment: chain mail, spear (two-handed) HP 5 ☐☐☐☐☐ 

### 6. Elfreda Thorne

Elfreda, which means “Elf Strength,” was a name to live up to—and she did. Disappointment in a female heir seems to be a Human phenomenon. Aelfric knew that by teaching his only daughter everything he knew from a young age, she would far exceed him over time. When that day finally arrived, he gave her his blessing to continue her education out in the world among peers. This stirred controversy and criticism, to which Aelfric sternly responded, “She is not my possession, but a gift.”

### 7. Muireall Whelani

She was born to a young mother and an elderly father. A former pirate and smuggler, he’d had a change of heart when the cargo turned out to be a Dwarven woman. Leaving instructions for his crew on his study table, he fled his anchored ship with the woman and his treasure in tow—rowing to the mainland, never to return. Changing his name and appearance, he reinvented himself as a blacksmith in a remote settlement. He ofered the woman freedom; she remained. A child was born. Weapons were forged in her name. Training was given. All was well until the slavers’ bandits came in search of the traitor. He’d planned for this day—the wagon, the weapons, the escape route, the means to start over. Sending a messenger boy with a red ribbon and a piece of silver, he met the bandits head on. Fighting with the ferocity that only a husband and father can truly know, he felled many before finally being overcome. The family lived on. Another name in another place; another chance. “Muireall” (“Sea-Bright”) contemplated revenge, but knew it would endanger her mother. She chose instead to become a warrior in her own right—one worthy of her father’s sacrifice. She is the half-sister of Máirín Nolani (page 49) though neither is aware of the other’s existence. If they were to ever meet and learn the truth, both might feel diferently about their father than they do now.

### 8. Ringo Grandhollow

Some fighters are made, not born. Ringo grew into his role slowly, first accepting it as a community responsibility to which he was delegated, before realizing that he had real talent for it, and eventually embracing it as part of his identity. It was the stark realization that there wasn’t enough for him to do at home that led him to wandering into the wide world, leaving his kin with mixed emotions of pride and regret.

### Magic-Users (1d8)

### 1. Wazo Forsbergs

”Wazo the Wildcard” was a mystery to everyone— friends, teachers, and especially his parents. The youngest in his family, it was expected that he would demonstrate some discernible talent at some point. But years passed and he achieved little, impressed no one, and irritated most. He read a lot and he did chores, but poorly enough so that he wouldn’t be asked to ever do them again. He was nearly 10 when he first saw Odovacar Alcimus Libo (page 115) who was then a younger man, but still thrilling to behold. Transfixed, Wazo used his literacy to voraciously consume anything he could find on the subject of magic. Then, he pestered wizards relentlessly. Many Magic-Users mentioned in this book will have either heard of or been accosted by Wazo. Those that know him will deny it so as to avoid further contact.

### 3. Pádraigín Mulloyi

While still in the dawn of his career, Pádraigín is already spending an inordinate amount of time daydreaming of a future he has hardly begun to earn. In his mind’s eye, he is already famous and the ruler of the area is ofering him a title of nobility if he agrees to build a stronghold within the ruler’s domain. Tickled by the invitation, he ponders whether he should reply immediately or in a week or two to inform the ruler that other rulers in outlying regions are also vying for his attention. Embarrassingly, he also casts magic mouth on his pocket mirror and asks daily, “Mirror, mirror, in my hand. Who’s the greatest wizard in the land?”

### Thieves (1d8)

### 1. Darby Caldwelle

Once a proud name in this family, Darby means “free from envy,” but in this case it fits like a pair of oversized, hand-me-down trousers from an elder relative. If Darby sees, he covets. If he covets, he takes. Embarrassed, his mother has started telling the neighbours that he died. In a sense perhaps he did, if the son she imagined ever actually existed.

### 2. Afton Brewstere

Young thieves tend to have big dreams and empty pockets. Afton’s plan is to own a ship someday, and make the leap from landlocked thievery to buccaneering. Ships cost more than you think though. And it’s not just the ship, it’s the equipment, maintenance, crew, food and drink, salaries, and bribes. No small expense! So it’s robbery on dry ground until those cofers fill up.

### 3. Cyril Beckette

An orphan and street urchin, Cyril envisions himself lord of his own guild someday. He understands hierarchy and is starting small, making it clear to children in the neighbourhood that he wants information and has the coin to pay for it. Early reports are mostly concerned with adultery and unlawful acts with livestock, so blackmail seems like the first viable income source, with extortion on the table for those that don’t choose to part with their money painlessly. Not yet comfortable with doing physical harm to clients, he’ll contract the work out until he develops the stomach for it. Speaking of which, it’s lunchtime—somewhere.

### 4. Giselmund Groosg

Giselmund—"one who is committed to protecting the hostages"—could not agree more with the sensibility of his name. Hostages are worth a lot of money, and should they escape or be taken by a rival that’s a lot of money lost—not to mention time and efort. To prevent this, he goes to great lengths to hide his captives in the most obscure locations—preferably remote, sound-dampened, or masked by busy trafic or other environmental noise. Abduction: a sensible business where the potential inventory never runs out. 

Dwarf Thief 1, AC 14, AB +1, #At 1 longsword, Dam 1d8, Mv 30', ML 9 STR 12, INT 10, WIS 9, DEX 13 (+1), CON 12, CHA 14 (+1) Equipment: leather armour, longsword HP 4 ☐☐☐☐ 

### 5. Canicus Soranus

As a youth, Canicus was frequently described as gentle, kind, and fair. Not one to take these sorts of insults lying down, he’s endeavouring to become a little worse each day. Wanton violence, cruelty and immorality are still a long way away, but he is determined to “un-improve” himself. He’s nowhere near ready to choose a specialization yet, so he’s open to trying anything you’ll pay him to do.

### 6. Flyta Elwyne

Flyta fancies herself as a peoples’ champion, robbing from the rich and giving to the poor. Harsh economic conditions have made this a dificult puzzle, with no wealthy individuals living anywhere near her home, and locals being distinguished in prosperity by the narrowest of margins. In fact, being robbed by her or having her spend her stolen income on their wares seems to be the most influential factor in whose fortunes go up and whose go down. Not ideal, but it beats a completely stagnant economy.

### 7. Regina Pictor

A pretty face opens doors, but also chests, satchels, and coin purses. While not the strongest thief, her delicate body, lithe movement, and charismatic personality rarely prevent her from getting what she wants. Unsurprisingly, men in particular seem to struggle the most—not physically, but mentally. Even the most learned find themselves stunned as their mind frantically processes the dichotomy between what they are being asked to do, and the woman asking them to do it. The shortsword is a last means of egress from the rare situation she can’t talk her way out of.

### 8. Kirsa Brauneg

Years of childhood play allowed Kirsa to perfect the art of the steal. Concealed in the branches of trees, good timing and a sleeper hold is all that lies between opportunity and payday. While the sweets and treats of yesteryear are gone, coins and occasional “sparkly rocks” make adult living a little easier to aford.

## Second-Level Characters

### Clerics (1d8)

### 1. Bertle “The Birdman” Bradleye

In nearly every regard, Bertle is the apex of disciples. Strong, resilient, intelligent, and wise, he is the man that many wish they could be, were it not for his appearance… and the smell. At any time, 1d6-1 birds of various types are perched on his arms, shoulders and head (1d4-1 indoors; 1d2- 1 while seated). Regardless of rolls, one crow will be present with him at all times. His friendship with birds might be charming if they didn’t expel their waste on him with such alarming regularity, or if Bertle had more concern for hygiene. But as they are his constant companions, messengers and protectors, he’s more likely to miss their company than yours. 

Human Cleric 2, AC 14, AB +1, #At 1 warhammer, Dam 1d6+1, Mv 30', ML 9 STR 15 (+1), INT 16 (+2), WIS 14 (+1), DEX 9, CON 17 (+2), CHA 8 (-1) Spells: light* Equipment: leather armour, shield, warhammer HP 12 ☐☐☐☐☐ ☐☐☐☐☐ ☐☐ 

![image](https://cdn-mineru.openxlab.org.cn/result/2026-09-15/807cc405-0084-4a45-8e24-5c7f50c4cc53/7ca978e64e4413fb1667237626ed44443f80a09e5c6dd199d7088cc35abb9ede.jpg)


### 2. Achaicus Spinther

If you know Achaicus Spinther, consider yourself fortunate indeed because you are part of a network of more people than you realize. It would be a stretch to say that Achaicus knows everyone personally, but he generally knows someone that knows the someone you’re inquiring about, and knows how you might get to know even more. Even if he doesn’t know who you’re talking about, a few words about their discipline or background is all he needs to know in order to reach out to the person who does. Far from being a know-it-all, Achaicus is acutely aware of how much there is to know and therefore how important it is to have a well- developed network of experts who know all the things you could never find the time to learn, let alone master. Get to know him—and, if you’re very lucky, his eldest brother, Themistocles Spinther (page 85). Oddly, despite their vast social and professional connections, both brothers flatly deny any involvement with the Coalition for Magical Cooperation. Publicly, at least. His favourite person to spend time with these days is Lavinia Septima (page 15). He’s finding that his mind is increasingly distracted by thoughts of her, and is not at all bothered by it. 

Human Cleric 2, AC 17, AB +1, #At 1 maul, Dam 1d10, Mv 20', ML 9 STR 11, INT 15 (+1), WIS 17 (+2), DEX 16 (+2), CON 14 (+1), CHA 16 (+2) Spells: cure light wounds* Equipment: chain mail, maul HP 11 ☐☐☐☐☐ ☐☐☐☐☐ ☐

### 3. Finnean O'Connor

Wherever Finnean goes, his dogs are always by his side. With one head under each hand more often than not, it’s like he stood up from an armchair and the armrests just followed. Entirely silent, they respond primarily to commands delivered by hand signals. They are large domesticated riding dogs and are occasionally ridden for transport or used for hauling cargo, as Halflings are known to do. He alternates which is ridden. Clearly there is some friendly rivalry between the pair, and Finnean goes to lengths to ensure that they know they are loved equally.

### 4. Earnan Ó Haonghuisi

Earnan seems from a distance to be a solitary man and, until recently, was often to be found silent and in his own company in the Temple of Daevas. They accepted him without asking a single question, and left him free to pursue independent study. Of all the places he’s studied, the Temple of Daevas contains his favourite libraries. He can get lost for hours learning about the many gods whose names are no longer spoken. It’s become less serene with the frequent comings and goings of a truly awful and profane group of four, led by Valentina Vibulanus (page 104). Soon after this began, angry and violent public protests followed in their wake. Now Earnan’s been asked to stop coming to the Temple. He was told it was for his own safety, but from what? None of the protesters ever laid a hand on him. Perhaps it’s Valentina. Without speaking a word, she radiates fury like he’s never felt, and he’s not usually sensitive to how people are feeling. People confuse him. Unlike books, they are dificult to read and you can’t close them up when you’ve had your fill. Also, they don’t like it when you put them on the shelf for a month or two while attending to more pressing things, like reading. Friendship has been elusive. Partnership is less satisfying, but more manageable. When they finally barred him from entering, Earnan put his name up on the notice board looking for work. He might be worth bringing along. So long as he is out on a mission and there are monsters to fight, he’s a fine man to have by your side. But in the awkward quiet before and afterwards...

### 5. Edda Hunte

If you’ve thought about the way a grandmother behaves and wondered, “Were they always this way?” meeting Edda at her present age might lead you to agree that they probably were. Concerned for others before herself, her first thought is always, “How can I make things better for everyone?” With clear and measurable daily goals, meticulously planned, everyone’s life at the Temple of Plenty is improved by her presence. Don’t ask her what she wants. Don’t suggest you’ll do something for her. You’ll receive a stern finger wagging, a polite rebuke, and a barelyconcealed smile. A better approach is to just do her a kindness anonymously and without permission. She’ll protest lightly that such things are completely unnecessary, with another flash of that elusive smile.

### 7. Lavinia Septima

Seventh daughter of a seventh daughter, Lavinia demonstrated rudimentary abilities to purify and heal from a tender age. A warrior in only the most minimalist sense, her actions in combat are exclusively in defence of the weak and in aid of the wounded. With greater powers and the right magical items, she would gratefully shed her armour, weapon and shield in order to manoeuvre more easily to heal the victims, while nullifying their attackers with appropriate spells. She’s recently been surprised by attention from Achaicus Spinther (page 13) and would like him more, if only he would open up about his family. Her sisters are everything to her, and he would need to feel the same way. It wouldn’t hurt if he had a social conscience and used his networking to improve the world, either. 

Human Cleric 2, AC 13, AB +1, #At 1 mace, Dam 1d8-1, Mv 30', ML 9 STR 6 (-1), INT 12, WIS 15 (+1), DEX 7 (-1), CON 9, CHA 16 (+2) Spells: purify food and water Equipment: leather armour, shield, mace HP 8 ☐☐☐☐☐ ☐☐☐ 

### 6. Thistle Leighe

Handsome but a bit prickly, Thistle is adjusting to the increased noise, speed, and congestion of city living. Obviously things were a bit too quiet back at home but is it really necessary to completely pack the day sunup to sundown with non-stop action, weird smells, incessant chatter, and people packed in the streets like kernels on an ear of corn? And then do it again the next day, only slightly diferently? WHAT IS WRONG WITH YOU ALL?! But don’t judge him too harshly. He’s only Halfling, after all. He fares better in the company of a small group of adventurers that he likes, where the purpose of the mission is clear and there’s time set aside for food, drink, a good laugh, and a proper night’s sleep.

### 8. Alberich Grosf

Alberich is becoming increasingly obsessed with Elves, and because he was always taught to revel in being a Dwarf, he’s feeling confusion mixed with guilt. He honestly can’t help it. He’s now questioning his decision to join the clergy and wondering whether it wouldn’t be better to woo and marry a lovely Elf woman, then raise a little brood of “Dwelves” in some picturesque little cabin. Is any of it possible? While he wrestles with this quandary, his spells aren’t working as reliably as they used to, which only adds to his shame.

### Fighters (1d8)

Eldest daughter of a barber, Ulla followed her own dream of being a Lumberjill (woodcutter) until she sufered a broken leg while leaping from tree to tree. Now fully healed and retrained as a fighter, she still favours her former tool in trade, albeit a “slightly larger” version. 

### 2. Hartmut Dreierg

### 1. Ulla Bösch

There have always been voices in Hartmut’s head, but he was always convinced they were still his own thoughts expressing themselves through diferent voices. Lately he’s not so sure. There are three distinct voices and he hopes that at least one of them is still his, or a manifestation of him—like his conscience. But what of the other two? His mother used to tell him about the Angel and the Devil that whispered into the ears of children, one perched on each shoulder. Perhaps it wasn’t just a story. If only he could ask someone without looking foolish, or worse, insane.

### 3. Kaelyn Hamme

As the youngest sibling of three older boys, Kaelyn learned to fight the way most boys do. Why wear stupid dresses when they make it hard to charge the muddy field, wooden sword in hand, to slay the dragon? Her brothers never feared her, but they received enough blows from the flat side of her blade to respect her. She demands the same of any potential party member. Any snide remarks about women being less able fighters than men will be met with an icy stare, more biting than a harsh retort. Revenge will come unexpectedly. Tricks, traps, and practical jokes were also part of the training syllabus at Hamme House.

### 4. Dubhshìth Donoghuei

Dubhshìth is settling into his second career after a rather disastrous attempt to serve as a Magic-User’s Apprentice. “But you’re a Dwarf,” the well-mannered Wizard said. “Dwarves can’t be Magic-Users.” But Dubhshìth lied, insisting he was really just a short and ugly Human. Naturally, there were the kinds of disasters one would expect when non-magically-attuned folk dabble in the impossible. The Wizard’s Tower was eventually repaired, but because some of the stones were blasted into the farms of a neighbouring county, there were many heated arguments between the rulers and afected parties about who should pay, and how much. Thank the Creator that the news scribes have stopped following. The scandal sold a lot of scrolls, but enough is enough already.

### 5. Viggo Praeconinus

When reminded that his name is Latin for “war” Viggo will roll his eyes and correct you that it’s only true for the root of his name, vig. Then he’ll point out that his complete first name is of Old Norse and Scandinavian origin and can mean, “battle,” “fight,” or “one who lies in a cradle.” Waiting for your surprised reaction, he will add, “Are you calling me a baby?” Then, without waiting for an answer, he’ll smile and return to his book, scroll, wood carving, or whatever diversion was consuming his attention before you so rudely interrupted. He is not a common fighter. He obeyed his parents and continued the lineage of warriors as was expected of him, but the job constitutes only the smallest fraction of a much richer inner life. Viggo fights with a ruthless eficiency of movement. Hack and slash is for the weak-minded. He lives economically and saves aggressively. He will build a keep as soon as it’s possible to do so, teach his deadly swordplay to his Captain of the Guard, and live out his days whittling, painting, reading, and perhaps even writing. Surely he could write better content than the authors of some of the truly terrible books he’s found along the roadside. And in private libraries. Magic-Users collect a surprising amount of trashy romance—and worse.

### 6. Eileanóra Yorke

On her 11th birthday, a fortune teller came by Eileanóra’s door to reveal her future. Seeing her father gripping his weapon in anticipation, the woman said, “A fighter. Yes, that’s it. Put down your dolls and pick up an axe instead.” Crestfallen, Eileanóra delivered her toys to the local orphanage, but her mood turned to delight in seeing the happy faces of the children receiving her gifts. She’d discovered her destiny on her own. Now grown and having acquired enough skill with an axe to please her father, she knows her real passion is social welfare. She’s established a small, grassroots circle of likeminded citizen contacts that watch the cities for children and families in dire need. When identified, they are passed on to established groups that are capable of intervening without fear of reprisal. She takes some pride in the success of her rescues. The girl now renamed Carlyn Donovani (page 9) is the most high profile example. One of her best grassroots network volunteers, Theodosius Scipio (page 28), is actually in love with her. He’s said nothing and she has no idea; she’s just grateful for the useful information he provides her nearly every day.

### 7. Brandr Halvard XIV

Brandr Halvard, 14th of his name, is not nearly as interesting as he believes he is. While intelligent and reasonably wise, he is fully devoted to ensuring that number 14 will go down in history as the greatest incarnation. Thus, a typical day with Brandr revolves around training, talking about training, fighting, and reminiscing about good fights. That’s no fun for anyone to listen to, and it can quickly devolve into cringe-inducing topics if Brandr overshares, which he very nearly always does. His dietary concerns based on his recent bowel movements is but one example— and we’ll stop right there. His sword, Gudbrand, is a family heirloom that he never removes and now even sleeps with. On more than one occasion, attempts have been made to steal it.

### 8. Melitta Merenda

When Melitta’s father stepped out to look in on his bees after supper one night, no one thought much about it. Dusk was always a good time to check; the risk of accidental stinging was much less. When he did not return before morning, she set of in search. His body had been partially eaten by a were-creature; it was unclear which. The gruesome discovery left a permanent scar. Thereafter, she was always cognizant of the moon cycles, and since adulthood has kept one or more protection scrolls on hand at all times.

### Magic-Users (1d8)

### 1. Sighilde De Vend

Fiercely independent and charismatic, some believe that she would have excelled as a fighter. They’re correct. But, like her elder siblings Aloysia (page 57) and Ortrud (page 111) she gravitated towards the arcane. Eschewing the pathetic and dated stylings of the wizards she remembers growing up with—weird-bearded geezers wearing their bed-covers around town— she took a diferent approach. It’s something akin to what Odovacar Alcimus Libo (page 115) might wear, if he weren’t also aging out of fashion: a smart tunic, a stylish vest with pockets for daggers and vials, and no creepy hat.

### 2. Sòlas Ó Caisidei

Watching his father’s troubles as a moneylender permanently shaped Sòlas’ worldview. He trusts no one, anywhere, to do anything agreed upon, ever—except his brother, Colla Ó Caisidei (page 59). If you’re anyone else he won’t loan his quill, agree to meet anywhere at a specific time, or trust that you feel the way you say you do, or that anything about your behaviour is anything other than a clever ruse to conceal some other real intent. He only trusts books—and only some of those—because they may have instructions that dictate actions that yield applicable results. Thus, he enjoys cooking and casting spells. He’d like to make potions, but the books suggest that he isn’t ready yet. He’ll keep studying. Please don’t bother him or he’ll bar you from the room. 

sketchingjohn

### 3. Gumarich Braddocke

It’s not clear how serious Gumarich is about being a Magic-User. The two spells he was most excited to learn are still the only two spells he’s ever learned, and he doesn’t seem motivated to work any harder. Conversations with the family dog where the dog talks back, and a mouth on the lavatory door that cheers loudly for the activities occurring within are just some of the hilarious hijinks this week—and every week—at the Braddocke family home. Bufoon.

### 4. Leopoldine Botwrighte

Leopoldine has always hated her name. It’s an ugly name. It doesn’t slide of the tongue. It stumbles—awkwardly. It doesn’t even have a good shortened form, except for Leo, but that’s for males… which leads to the reason that she hates her name the most. She fears that she’s a disappointment to her father. He’d set his mind to expect another boy and he got her instead. She’s never felt like he loved her any less. It’s just a feeling. And brother Stanford Botwrighte (page 26), with his plate mail and a party already doing weekend dungeon delving fills Papa with a pride you can see all over his face. It’s made her feel like she has to be one of those knifethrowing wizards, like the one they saw at the Fair last year, so she’s started practising in secret. 

Human Magic-User 2, AC 10, AB +1, #At 1 dagger, Dam 1d4, Mv 40', ML 9 STR 9, INT 14 (+1), WIS 11, DEX 8 (-1), CON 11, CHA 10 Spells: magic missile, charm person Equipment: 3 old daggers HP 5 ☐☐☐☐☐ 

### 5. Hilda Del Bosques

The product of an extremely strict upbringing, Hilda feels relief that she is finally at a point in her life where she can make her own choices. At the top of the list: what to do with these powers she has? When her brother Eoforwine (page 60) embraced thievery as his trade, it sent shockwaves through the family. “We raised you better than this,” they said, before turning their backs on him and barring any communication. But is it really wrong to use the skills you have to get the things you want? Her brother doesn’t think so and suggested that she might use her powers to make her life better—with her roommate, for example. Subtle applications of charm have improved that relationship substantially, and for those times that things are truly terrible, putting her to sleep is a welcome relief. There’s nothing wrong with that, is there?

As the first of their kin to manifest any magical abilities, the Fenne girls—Minna, Athela (page 70) and Lioba (page 99)—often feel like they have no one to turn to except each other. They’re grateful for that much, but would be ever more grateful if a well established family would reach out to them. It seems like everyone already knows each other, or at least knows of the family reputation. Their reputation is being made now.

### 7. Paschalis Sacerdos

As the only son of a live-in Mage Advisor to a ruler, Paschalis enjoyed a childhood of privilege, safe inside castle walls. So it continued, until the enchantress Valda Duerrg (page 86) set her eyes on that life for herself. Through beauty and sorcery, she warped the ruler’s mind until he fully believed he was surrounded by traitorous usurpers. Scores were put to the guillotine, including his parents. Valda solidified her position in becoming the ruler’s consort, but within the year he withered from an unknown ailment, still too paranoid to allow the Clerics anywhere near him. She remains safely within the walls of his castle. Paschalis learns, and waits for his chance.

### 8. Hartmut Glasse

At least within certain families, there’s an awful lot of pressure on young Magic-Users to go out adventuring. Why? Clearly, no one’s paying attention to the number of fatalities. Hartmut is —and he’s heard the stories. He even saw a body once. It’s lethal out there! Why would anyone choose to go slogging through stinky, slippery, vermin-infested labyrinths when there are plenty of great desk jobs for talented people in the city? He wouldn’t, except he feels like there’s no other choice. When his mother had a family portrait made last year, Hartmut’s first thought was, “Well, at least she’ll have something to look at wistfully as she tells the neighbour about her poor, fallen child for the 90th time.”

### 1. Fionghan Ennisi

Who doesn’t enjoy a good laugh? Fionghan once relied on humour to get through some very dificult times, but then forgot to stop. He has no regard for time and place. It’s quite unsettling to hear him gufaw—at work, in the temple, next to a burial site. Nobody’s laughing now. What few friends he has left are wondering if an exorcism is necessary. Recently, Fionghan’s heard of an Elf Magic-User with the same last name and jokes that he’s his cousin. That’s almost funny—but no —more like his inverse doppelganger. ### 2. Farvald Beckete

While he’s a much better burglar now than he used to be, mistakes on the job turned out to be a blessing that informed his technique. While on the run from a botched assignment, he took refuge in a warehouse where he found a Halflingsized shipping crate. Safely hidden inside, he was never discovered. Then the thought occurred, why not travel this way? And while Halflings are particularly good at hiding anyway, no one has been able to move between towns completely undetected as well as Farvald. The secret is the label. Always write the contents as something obviously important, but that no one is particularly excited to open upon receiving.

### 3. Étaín “The Face” Macginnisi

Étaín can’t really remember a time where he felt feelings for anyone. Any at all. He’s never hated anyone, but he’s never loved anyone either. But, he’s carefully observed people expressing emotions and noted how they either improve or damage relationships. He reads faces very well, and has learned how to emulate those faces and the words he’s heard to get the outcomes he desires. Casual acquaintances remark that he is one of the calmest, friendliest and most inquisitive people they’ve met. He uses this demeanour to build trust and collect information before stealing very particular items, and not many from any one person. He sticks around long enough to express his feigned concern, then quietly disappears from their lives.

### 4. Aston Paytone

His gifted dexterity made Aston’s choice of thievery simple, but uninterrupted success eventually made it desperately dull. So, one day, he stole an entire wardrobe of clothes from a particularly wealthy and famous person and proceeded to rob the next string of victims in the guise of that same celebrity. Moreover, before departing, he allowed himself to be seen—by waving to victims and bystanders—just to ensure everyone was thoroughly astonished and confused. While there was considerable doubt that a wealthy celebrity would pillage other peoples’ homes, it was such a delightful thing to imagine that the rumours continued to persist. Repeating the routine with new disguises finally established that it had all been a clever trick, but did nothing to diminish the delight it created, even among some of those that were robbed.

### 5. Osgar Humes

Even by the rather forgiving standards of Halfling behaviour, Osgar is arguably a big glutton. As a young recruit of the trade, he was considerably thinner and more dexterous. Unfortunately, the spoils of his adventures are going straight into steak, gravy, potatoes, and stout for breakfast, lunch, supper, plus those other in-between meals. Or, perhaps it’s fortunate. Does the world really need more thieves? And, prison would slim him down again.

### 6. Bríd Jekylle

Unpredictability can often be a plus when you’re a thief. Observable patterns are likely to be noticed. Then, plans are made, traps are set, and the thief caught. However, Bríd’s behaviour is more than just calculated misdirection. She seems truly incapable of managing her whims and reactions, particularly her temper. She can morph from a sweet, song-voiced angel to a vengeful, poison-spitting demon in short order. Worse still, the higher the stakes, the more likely she is to be emotionally unstable.

### 7. Alodie Weaver

Estranged sister of Edison (page 78). He had turned a blind eye to her activities until she used her relationship with him for an opportunity to empty the temple cofers. He paid for the losses from his own pocket, but warned her that any further losses he incurred by her actions would be met with a dispatch of assassins, gladly paid. There was a time when she would have doubted his sincerity, but there’s been a marked change since his career within the clergy has grown.

### 8. Woodrow Broadbente

Woodrow has learned that his best chance at success has been to set others up to fail. It’s nothing personal; it’s a necessity. Power is a hierarchy, with positions diminishing the closer you get to the top. You’re a small obstacle now, or a larger one later. Better to put you in your rightful place while the job is easier. On the ladder to success, it’s smart to climb hard, skip any rungs possible, and kick the faces of those climbing underneath you.

### Clerics (1d8)

The very model of frugality, Bathilda knows how to stretch a copper piece to acquire food and textiles, and not a scrap of it is wasted. She is not a miser, but a good-hearted soul who has had to turn away too many people because of the Temple of Want’s recurring shortages of food and clothing.

### 2. Bjarne Beckett

Bjarne was never really certain about a career in the clergy, but they were such nice folks! The Temple of Lohmen seemed as good a place as any to discover if he was meant to be a Cleric. Many of those who are lost discover their purpose here. After a promising start, he is now in the midst of a crisis of faith. His belief is eroding, his spells are failing, and his colleagues and mentors are concerned. 

Halfling Cleric 3, AC 18, AB +2, #At 1 warhammer, Dam 1d6+1, Mv 20', ML 9 STR 15 (+1), INT 7 (-1), WIS 13 (+1), DEX 12, CON 15 (+1), CHA 13 (+1) Spells: light*, remove fear* Equipment: plate mail, shield, warhammer HP 7 ☐☐☐☐☐ ☐☐ 

### 3. Imme Färberg

Imme, a former agnostic and lifelong skeptic, is aware that religion is deeply divisive among people. She’s thought about it deeply and accepts this as part of her mission. She wonders if the Temple of Judgment is really the right place to be having these feelings. She’ll never forget that before she believed in something, she was already a good person. She’s also met plenty of self-professed Deity-fearing men who—outside of the temple—shed their thin guise of decency and morality. She does good for all who are in need. No strings, no questions asked. Those who want to know more about her beliefs always ask her. Those who don’t, develop a deep respect for her despite the theological divide. She is a friend of Oswald Ashworthe (page 33). They meet up regularly to discuss local politics, philosophy and magic as a tool for social justice.

### 4. Stanford Botwrighte

Stanford wears a smile like a thief wears a disguise—to project an image that he hopes others will believe in. Maybe he can convince himself at the same time? He never had the athleticism to be a hardcore fighter. In fact, he feels like a person with marginal abilities across the board, but it’s surprising how much confidence a smile can inspire. He charmed them at the Temple of the Dire Wolf to get this clerical position—and the spells finally seem to be working now. And then there were the lunkheads hanging around the catacombs that were only too pleased to bring someone on who might help protect them from the skeletons. But at his core, he is terrified. He doesn’t want to die, but he doesn’t want to disappoint either; his Papa's obvious pride in him only makes things worse. His sister Leopoldine (page 21) has yet to see him fail and probably assumes that it comes easily to him to play the role of hero. In reality, he feels like a hostage of his own reputation. His remove fear spell is oftentimes for himself because the smile doesn’t always work. He knows it’s fake.

DEX 10, CON 9, CHA 13 (+1) 

Spells: protection from evil*, remove fear* Equipment: plate mail, shield, warhammer, map to 1d4 magic items HP 12 ☐☐☐☐☐ ☐☐☐☐☐ ☐☐ 

### 5. Sylva Demetra

Sylva has always been rather introverted, so when her personality changed, no one noticed. So far, she hasn’t either. She is the second member of the Temple of Worms to have been borrowed by a member of the Vitni, a noncorporeal race that learns by temporarily inhabiting physical beings. They strive to be as non-invasive as possible, and for the most part try to limit themselves to monitoring the host’s senses to observe activity. However, where they deem it necessary, they are capable of assuming temporary control to follow a path of interest. This may be as simple as asking a question or as arduous as steering the person through some desired experience (e.g. an adventure). Short losses of control are not usually noticed by the host, but long ones run the risk of host and Vitni asserting control simultaneously. From an outsider’s perspective, this looks a lot like seeing a person who is distracted or confused. In such cases, the host is unaware of why they are struggling to do what they intend and are unaware of the passing time. They sense they are internally conflicted, but do not sense the foreign presence. They become frightened when discovering they are doing things they did not choose to do. Hosts tend to assume they are experiencing the onset of dementia. Consequently, they often attempt to compensate as well as they are able in order to save face. Her elder Wilton Waters (page 64) is experiencing this now. It may be possible to drive the Vitni away if two Clerics cast Dispel Good and Dispel Evil simultaneously on an afected person.

### 6. Heide Foxe

If she wasn’t so lovable, she’d be the party member everyone hated. As a compromise, they hate how much they love her. Why? Heide loves animals to an immoderate degree. In her mind, the great outdoors are just an extension of her backyard... which is an extension of her living room. Wherever she goes there are furry creatures to befriend, adopt, take on the road as her travelling companions, and eventually take home. But, as she isn’t home often and it’s impossible to get a good pet sitter, her nonanimal companions have plenty of company on their journeys.

### 7. Bertrand Fishere

If there’s still daylight and he’s not otherwise occupied with oficial duties, Bertrand is out on the streets as a one-man recruitment team for the Temple of Judgment. No one asked him to do this. Word of this hasn’t reached the Temple— yet. His colleagues might be supportive if it weren’t for the fact that he asks prospective applicants to mention his name if they decide to join. He’s not out for a promotion or any sort of personal glory. He’s worried. He’s done terrible things in his past that he has yet to confess to. At the very least, he hopes the Deity will see his good work and forgive him. But, it’s his deeper hope that one of his mentors will pull him aside and ask for an explanation. It’s then that he will confess fully, and beg forgiveness from someone with the authority to grant it. He also accepts the possibility he might be expelled from the clergy. It’s what he fears he probably deserves anyway.

He bears both physical and psychological scars from those days of activism. ### 2. Theodosius Scipio

Ever since he first laid eyes on Eileanóra Yorke (page 18), Theodosius knew he loved her. She is strong, beautiful, and with a caring heart. What would it take for her to feel the same? Right now, he’s just one of many members of her grassroots network keeping an eye out for problems in the town. But, as a member of the town guard, he has occasion not only to see a lot more, but also hear more—including backroom discussions by his colleagues. He can’t imagine that any leak might be traced back to him. In any case, he’s certain that Eileanóra would only use the information to do good, and if there’s any chance she might grow to love him the way he loves her, it’s worth it. He doesn’t suspect that his Captain Cesan Finnegani (page 53) has dispatched his cousin Greysen Adaire (page 69) to tail him for suspicious behaviour.

### 3. Jarl Reidar

Beyond raw beefiness, Jarl never demonstrated much promise as a child. He was not very strong, not very fast, nor too wise, and only marginally good looking. However, his inability to read people or situations well did result in many fights during his youth. He learned a few things from being knocked to the ground, and now falls considerably less. He’s a challenging person to like. He speaks his uninformed opinions with pride and volume. He’s as emotionally blunt as the axe he drags around, and swings as cleanly. He lacks insight into himself and is completely unaware how events—particularly his own behaviour—afect others. If they knew each other, Afton Airaldii (page 67) might take him on as a fixer-upper. There’s got to be a redemption story there somewhere.

A likeable person when he’s sober, Stígandr is quietly slipping deeper into alcoholism. He’s unusually sensitive, though reticent to show this side of himself. He hates himself for it but feels trapped.

### 6. Ennius Falco

Ingenious by fighter standards—and by any standard—Ennius nonetheless finds himself in trouble when his reach exceeds his grasp. He's able to formulate intricate plans with plausibly good outcomes while failing to see the obvious pitfalls. Still, he’s often able to convince people to go along with his schemes and he’s been able to cope with “unforeseen events” due to his abilities, and no small amount of luck. To his great credit, he’s not an egomaniac. He’s never so enthralled by his own ideas to demand people to come along, and is happy to listen and follow a sensible plan from someone else.

### 7. Tronde Rolk

The quintessential halfling, young Tronde is relentlessly cheerful, reliable, and cooperative. His parents would not have taken him for an adventuring type but his remarkable strength, quickness and wisdom made him a standout to even the casual observer. When an opportunity came calling by way of a party passing through, he was happy to follow. If it’s no fun, there’s always home to come back to.

### 8. Hadubert Eklunds

With his many talents, Hadubert might have done anything—and still might. While his family expected him to do whatever he chose to the highest standard he could, he was free to choose. He dabbled in everything. He read, he fought, he spoke at length with as many elders as would spare him time. Extroverted and witty, he can captivate a room with tales of his party’s exploits with a style approaching that of epic literature. In battle, he is calm but lethal. He fights with fairness and grace, even against opponents who clearly wouldn’t ofer him the same courtesy. Secretly, he carries remorse for a misstep made as a younger man. Rather than consume him, he’s made it a lesson to ensure that he never wavers from the straight path.

### Magic-Users (1d8)

### 1. Petrina Mento

Logical to a fault, Petrina leaves emotion out of any problem solving or decision making process. This works... until it harms people and situations where emotional damage was one of the potential outcomes. Logic is her shield. She is both uncomfortable with people and emotions, and aware of the limits of her own intellect. So, she tends to boil situations down oversimplistically to reduce the number of variables to consider, and she tries to make herself sound smarter by giving overly complex explanations others are unlikely to understand. She’s never deliberately deceptive in circumstances where lives might be lost and she doesn’t seem evil. Perhaps it’s immaturity or insecurity. There’s still time for her to work on herself. She could use a better mentor.

### 5. Baylee Cookee

Sweet and bubbly, she’s not to everyone’s taste. In small amounts she is exactly the personality to lift you from your doldrums, but it can easily grow excessive. It’s unclear whether she really is a fountain of infinite happiness or whether it’s an act to make people like her. It’s backfiring. No one can be this positive all the time. ### 6. Imogene Ashworthe

Daughter of a King, her speech and mannerisms are on a completely diferent level than that of her peers. She’s told no one of her royal heritage and her name is of her own invention. It has spurred endless curiosity from Oswald Ashworthe (page 33), who’s desperate to establish how they might be distantly related. It’s too late to change now; she already established the new identity before they’d met. After the death of her mother she watched her father slip into crippling mistrust bordering on paranoia. Loving and empathetic, she did what he could until his controlling suspicion turned on her. Already aware that she had both magical powers and the afection of her subjects, she quietly slipped away in the middle of the night. Her father sent men in search, but the numbers dropped as time went on and morale plummeted. She’s kept nothing from her old life, but her time in the public eye carries the risk that she might be identified by a passing traveller.

### 7. Oswald Ashworthe

Bright, studious, and curious, it was these qualities that influenced his decision to veer away from his family’s warrior heritage. Too many beatings from his brothers might have also factored in—he was always the slowest of the bunch. It’s all for the best though. He has the physical body of a fighter, if not the inclination. He’s the sort of wizard that no one would dare pick a fight with, and shouldn’t. He owns a dagger, but uses it to open his mail or tend to his fingernails—which are always immaculately clean. He’s grappled, pinned or tossed enough troublemakers at the pub for people to know not to misbehave around him. He hates being alone, so he’s generally seen having tea or a stout with friends several times a week. He’s missing his family since his life changed, so was intrigued when he made the acquaintance of Imogene Ashworthe (page 32). She is elegant and efusive but becomes strangely guarded when asked about her own family ties. Perhaps he could do a bit of research in the library or consult the temple records. Surely Imme Färberg (page 25) could get him access there.

### Thieves (1d8)

### 1. Machar Shannoni

No powder, potion, food or drink invigorates Machar like the ridiculously dangerous risk-taking behaviours that he frequently engages in, just to make himself feel that way. Yes, stealing is fun for the income it brings in, but it’s nearly a fringe-benefit for Machar who feeds on the energy that the uncertainty always brings. With each job, he tries to barely escape, leaving himself less and less opportunity to extract himself successfully with the loot. Eventually he’ll want to get caught, then miraculously escape from custody. It’s concerning, but it’s the way he wants it.

### 2. Guirmean Kermitm

Never a hardcore thief, he steals to live but he would much rather make his living doing something reliable for pay—so long as the pay is good. He also doesn’t like to get dirty, and would prefer to avoid getting too much sun—it makes him sneeze. He likes to socialize with people but isn’t much of a team player. He also isn’t a good fit for repetitive work. The more he repeats something, the poorer his attention is to it. So, things like cleaning or manufacturing are right out. He’s never been a very good cook and is completely tone deaf. He’s afraid of the water, and even avoids bathing if he can help it. No magical talents to speak of. Can’t read. Can’t write. Can’t fight. Bad with money. Doesn’t believe in any deities. It’s odd that he’s having dificulty changing professions.

Nestor is given to make snap judgments about other people and their motives. He sizes people up at first sight or first word—usually both—and nothing can really change his mind once it’s been made. You’d think that he would learn, after people fail to disappoint him in the way that he expects. ### 4. Fleta Kendrick

Fleta, a Merchant-Thief, is an aspiring artist with no artistic talent. She also lacks the tools to practice with, and the will to learn even if she did have the tools. What’s been easier is stealing works of art from real artists and then passing them of as hers. It’s been challenging because she has to move frequently: stealing from one town, then selling in another. Experience has demonstrated that even this only works when the towns are very far apart because some artists’ styles are recognizable within the region. Also, in order to stave of requests for custom work, she’s had to invent a very eccentric personality that pretends that anything not currently on hand is passe. At whatever point the travel gets to be too much, she thinks she could retire comfortably as the owner/operator of a vanity gallery. Many have begged her to show their work alongside “her” art.

### 5. Bjarne Torsten

Bjarne wasn’t raised around other Halflings. He was a foundling, discovered in a box on the side of the road. His adoptive father was a merchant who first tried to sell him, unsuccessfully, before later considering that he might be a long-term investment if he could be made to work someday. Thus, Bjarne learned to speak by watching his father haggle with strangers from sunup to sundown every day of the week. As a result, Bjarne seems to argue for the sake of arguing, to either begin or prolong a conversation, and he always insists on having the last word. Many have tried to understand why he does this and set him straight—it just foments another argument. He can’t help it. Regarding language and interpersonal relations, it seems completely normal to him.

### 6. Abelia Cato

Abelia was raised in an orphanage and was never adopted. She is pleasant in appearance and has a quiet, calming voice, but she is reluctant to use it. She keeps to herself, avoids eye contact with others, and generally doesn’t ever speak unless spoken to first. Even when asked a question directly, she tends to answer with brief, vague responses. She will often hint at what she wants or leave clues for others to find, rather than asking directly. Thievery has been both a means of survival due to poverty, and a way to get things she might freely be given if she were able to ask.

### 7. Heath Lyndone

Thievery was perhaps not the most inspired choice Heath made. Excelling at nothing in particular, he gravitated toward what appeared to require the least efort. He liked that it was light on reading and allowed him to learn by doing. It was also nice to be able to work alone and set his own hours. And best of all, he could just decide not to come to work if he didn’t feel like it. But in addition to being too lazy to excel, Heath lacks the organization to succeed. He’s not great at coming prepared and refuses to make a checklist. He’s often forgotten his tools at home, left them in the places he’s burgled, or misplaced them entirely with no clue when they were last seen. He’s clumsy and given to hysterics and expletives when injured on the job. He’s easily frustrated, and locks are really quite a bit trickier than he thought they'd be. Also, the tools are expensive to keep replacing and the guy he buys used sets from laughed at him the last time he came in for another one. Too embarrassed to ever return, Heath keeps an eye out for scrap metal by the Blacksmith’s anvil.

### 8. Pantera Lupus

Pantera, who hasn’t worked in many months, is concerned about her health but is having a hard time being taken seriously. Her symptoms are real, but varied, with aflictions to her skin, eyes, and joints that make it impossible to do her job. The healers are useless. They’ve written her of as a hypochondriac, suggesting that she’s inventing her illness but unable to make up a sensible one. Others have suggested she’s going insane. What quacks! After waiting for what she considered a suficient amount of time to allay suspicion, she broke into their stores and robbed them of all their medicines. She’d like to try to treat herself, if she knew what all these powders and pastes were for. There’s got to be someone who believes her, and will at least try to help.

## Fourth-Level Characters

### Clerics (1d8)

### 1. Vesper Calussa

Vesper has reached a friction point as a Cleric. She (alternately, he) does enjoy the feeling of not being utterly lost and confused all the time. She appreciates that spells work most of the time and as intended. It’s the crushing expectation that she must learn and pray and pray and learn, all day and night, that is now utterly exhausting. There must be a shortcut to progress somewhere, where one does not need to concern themselves with mundane tangents and pointless minutia. The structure and routine; the constant oversight, mentoring and accountability—it’s all a bit much. She’s heard that the Temple of Daevas has a pretty low bar for entry. The rumour also suggested that it had closed its doors to the public, but surely they wouldn’t turn away a new recruit ready to pledge service to their cause… whatever that is.

### 2. Huberta Bushe

Huberta is still finding her way. Not a leader and not a follower, she’s feeling out her environment to determine who she might trust. A true believer, it’s organized religion that leaves her skeptical. She is concerned by what is rumoured to be the real intent of the Temple of Eternal Life. The clergy elite are rarely seen and never spoken of, except in whispers. From the limited vantage point of her current station, everything seems fine, but it sure doesn’t feel that way. The biographical information of some of the senior members doesn’t make sense, unless extreme longevity is more common than she knew. If only she could access the lower levels. There must be better records down there. Living quarters? Meeting rooms? Something.

### 3. Brianag Mohani

When she was younger, Brianag met a priestess that completely changed her view of what clergy could be like. She wasn’t old, stufy and buttoned up like most were. Her name was Whitney Worthame (page 108) and she worked for the Temple of Worms. “Gross!” Brianag had said. “Yes!” Whitney agreed, not missing a beat. She then told the younger girl how worms make beautiful things grow, even where the land had been poisoned. “That could be you, someday, Brianag,” she concluded. “Making the world better when others feel like it’s hopeless.” That conversation changed Brianag’s life. She knew what she wanted to do with her life. Accepting the invitation to become a Worm, Brianag now has her faith but also a personal code of conduct that guides her in the absence of rules. One of the things she’s still learning from Whitney is how to cope with circumstances where the rules get in the way.

### 4. Cassandra Flamen

The Flamen family have been priests for the entirety of living memory and historical record. The name is so synonymous with the role that some believe that the role was named after the family, “flamine” being a foreign word for priest. Naturally it was assumed that Cassandra would continue in that tradition. She’s gone along with it so far, securing a position at the Temple of the Morning Star while she grows in faith and power. But she’s a long way away from where her own ancestors filled the role, and while she is new to the area, her name is not. She’s gotten the sense that she is unwelcome here and that other family names seeking to make their own mark on this Temple would rather see her quietly disappear. There’s been nothing threatening, but the eerie insularity has made her fear that it was wrong of her to come this way. As an only child, she never considered what would happen to the family line were she to die without bearing children. Now it weighs on her heavily.

She showed early promise as a beginning acolyte, but now Sunna is feeling the pressure to maintain her standing. Her frazzled nerves and characteristic enthusiasm are now stepping on toes as she tries to remain at the forefront. Increasingly, she answers for other people rather than letting them speak. Right or wrong, it’s awkward for everyone in attendance and it’s poisoning her friendships. ### 8. Gaheris Allobrogicus

A recent immigrant to the area, Gaheris is still struggling both with understanding and making himself understood. His grasp of the local dialect is improving but the frequent use of idioms and slang still causes him a lot of confusion, as does humour. Consequently, he’s found himself very reliant on those around him to explain some meanings more clearly, and in some cases, even to make decisions on his behalf. It’s ripe for manipulation for anyone who might want to cause him harm, or just use him for their own purposes. Gaheris is similarly out of touch with the regional culture, and even the history of the Temple of Oreh. Hopefully he’s in the right place and surrounded by the right kind of people. Nothing bad has happened—so far.

### Fighters (1d8)

### 1. Emerson Bloodworthe

Though not a stellar fighter by any measure, Emerson feels invincible. On the field this looks a lot like bravery, until you look closer and see the degree of risk he undertakes for the amount of skill he possesses. There’s clearly some kind of disconnect there between his perception and reality. It’s a lifelong condition. Voted by his school classmates as “Most Likely to Acquire a Career-Ending Permanent Injury,” Emerson just interpreted it as a challenge to be overcome before the reunion party. At least he’s got a good shield.

### 2. Maon Mcclellandi

Now retired from military service, Maon has begun taking on jobs as a mercenary. For a while he enjoyed the freedom of travelling light, meeting new people, and filling his schedule any way he pleased. It still has its moments. Warm and gregarious, he has the capacity to be the life of the party should he find himself in a pub of like-minded strangers. It’s the quiet hours that are killing him. He never lived alone before and now realizes he hates it. In the military, his troop served double as friends and family. Now that’s all gone. He needs to find a new one.

### 3. Alberic Rufinus

Alberic (“Elf Ruler”) has probably missed his chance to live up to his name. Life was idyllic in his family at the time that it was bestowed, but the call to war claimed his father while he was still a child. When the invaders came to sack the castle, Alberic was already gone with his mother, living anonymously under her brother’s roof. There is no castle to reclaim, just a ruin, though he could rebuild a new one there if he’s successful as a fighter. He has the talent—few are quicker with a blade. But he also has the strategic mind. While he is a very good tactician when given the time to plan an attack, where he really shines is in formulating a new strategy on the field when things have gone wrong. Almost instinctively, he can see how events will unfold based on the decisions he makes in the moment. He wonders if this is something inherited from his father, or something of his very own.

### 5. Wassa Fairburne

Wassa has a strange way of dealing with anxiety. If there is ever anything upsetting to her, be it an unfortunate event or an interpersonal incident— she pretends it never happened. Heated arguments, disappointing jobs, sudden deaths— never happened. It might have been a clever coping mechanism at one time, but it’s gone on too long now. Talking with her requires that you remain aware of two realities—hers and everyone else’s. You can try to confront her with evidence that her recollections of events are wrong. ### 6. Máirín Nolani

The half-sister of Muireall Whelani (page 6), she never knew her father and is unaware of her sibling’s existence. He had already disappeared before she was born and so she was given her mother’s last name. Her first name, Máirín—“star of the sea”—is the only thing that connects her to her father and half-sister. In her mind, he is still alive somewhere, sailing in search of riches. She briefly gave some thought to becoming a pirate herself, but was then sobered by the reality of being trapped on a ship with a dozen angry men. So, in the midst of her work in the information trade, she listens for any mention of him. It’s entirely possible that his name will pass over Muireall’s lips and be overheard by someone in the network. How would it feel to know she had more living family? Would they feel drawn together, or angered by each other? It would be a dificult experience for each of them to understand how one man could be so diferent to two of his children. 

Dwarf Thief 4, AC 14, AB +2, #At 1 battle axe, Dam 1d8, Mv 30', ML 9 STR 11, INT 15 (+1), WIS 8 (-1), DEX 15 (+1), CON 13 (+1), CHA 13 (+1) Equipment: leather armour, battle axe HP 16 ☐☐☐☐☐ ☐☐☐☐☐ ☐☐☐☐☐ ☐ 

### 7. Payton Hawarde

Payton has raised thievery to a high art, like sculpture or composing a symphony. She (alternately, he) is a meticulous planner with high standards and a tendency to criticize her performance overly harshly. She replays her mistakes over and over in her mind, and tries to better the performance. Oddly, this makes her priorities completely reversed to that of her current peers, who are pleased with even a sloppy job so long as it is successful. Payton has begun taking on jobs not because she desires a particular item so much as because it’s another opportunity to practice.

### 3. Ellanher Bergströms

Wary of warnings from her family that she might meet her end the way Icarus did, Ellanher sought to curb her risky, thrill seeking behaviour by joining an organization in direct opposition. The Temple of the Dead Moth is a very old sect preaching the hazards of just that sort of lifestyle. Admittedly, it’s turned her life around. On a daily basis she gets to meet people just like she was, some of whom are much more damaged by their own reckless living. They remain in the safety of the temple from anywhere from a day to a few months as they recover. When they leave, a few come back to express thanks for the help they received at the Temple. Some return for another stay when they've strayed again into errant living. Entirely too many come back in a shroud for burial. Ellanher is one of the thankful.

### 2. Murchadh Innes

The determination of a Dwarven Fighter has two sides. Murchadh has the epic fighter side that refuses to give up when the odds aren’t favourable and the likelihood of victory—if not survival—is diminishing. He persists, and people love him for it. But he also has the epic Dwarf side that refuses to give up absolutely anything at all, ever, under any circumstances once his mind is made up about it. “No” is a word he doesn’t like to hear—and so he doesn’t. He’ll tell himself you were joking; he’ll tell himself you didn’t understand; he’ll tell himself he knows better, and then proceed accordingly. He’s a good lad if you keep him engaged in battle and away from the conversation.

Sassa has known many vile men like Cesan Finnegani (page 53) and Greysen Adaire (page 69) in her day. They’ve never heard of her, but she feels like she knows them well by the way that others have spoken about them behind their backs. Whether they realize it or not, a bad reputation follows you everywhere, invisibly. If Sassa could hear what is said about her, it wouldn’t change her behaviour, but at least she’d feel some assurance that the rumour mill communicates the good with the bad. ### 5. Beowulf Wilmere

As a hero-for-hire, Beowulf has found himself in some terrifying life-and-death situations, but he honestly believed the day he met Leofsige Alvine (page 114) was going to be the last day of his short life. He was browsing produce in the marketplace when the gag went in his mouth and the shroud enveloped his body. The bumpy wagon ride couldn’t end soon enough. He hoped the end would be quick. Instead, he found himself deposited at the centre of a room, encircled by 7 robed figures holding wands and weapons. One spoke. The voice told a story of four unearthly beings—The Tetrad—walking among us draped in the guises of men and women. Disappearances, deaths, a possible prelude to an invasion—it sounded surreal. Then Leofsige approached, knelt, and introduced himself. Beowulf had been chosen for his abilities, but more so, for his character. He’d been closely surveilled for months. As a junior member of a covert group of overseers—The Octad—he would need to do nothing more than continue his profession, passing along what he saw and heard—now that his attention had been turned.

### 7. Osborn Torgeir

Osborn broke his left big toe walking through a doorway once. It festered, turned gangrenous, and had to be cut of and the wound cauterized. He’s never walked the same way again—through doorways. Otherwise, he’s perfectly fine. The doorway to his home is now protected by a small symbol of his chosen Deity. He only enters and exits through the front door so that his Deity’s protection both enters and leaves with him. He’s heard that the back door might also be safe if similarly equipped, but he’s just not ready to risk it. He always enters right-foot-first, exits the same way, and demands that any visitors do the same. He also won’t shake hands with anyone standing in his doorway because local Lore-givers told him it will make a friend into an enemy. Now he wants to ask whether it might turn an enemy into a friend.

When Truinnean was a boy he was told never to lie, but when he told the truth terrible things were done to him—shouting, beatings, and even worse punishments. ### Magic-Users (1d8)

### 1. Cynebald Bosques

Among his peers, Bosques is regarded as one of the most talented Masters for young Magic-Users of first or second level. Such is his influence that an apprentice’s career can be nearly destroyed by a poor review. For example, Anencletus Macer (page 8), a former student, was dismissed for snooping through Bosques’ personal spellbooks and research materials. Even those who’d never met Bosques agreed to close ranks, indefinitely eliminating Anencletus from consideration for future apprenticeships. While strict, Boques is generous with his time, honest and fair, and earnest and respectful students have nothing to fear and much to gain from his tutelage. He is a member of the famed Coalition for Magical Cooperation.

### 2. Aloysia De Vend

With zero interest in life as a merchant, following in her brother’s footsteps was the easiest decision she ever made. He helped and guided her through the hurdles; a favour she would gladly pay down to her sister Sighilde (page 20) if she was the least bit receptive. However, it seems like Ortrud (page 111) is faltering. He doesn’t speak hopefully about a future in wizardry and wonders what else he might do instead. He’s even talked about returning to Father’s store. Nonsense. He just needs to find a better class of Magic-Users to associate with. She’s heard of at least one group working to make things better. It’s small, but growing. One of its leaders, Blossom Averye (page 113), is as powerful as he is. Would she accept his help, or be threatened by him? As a more junior Magic-User, Aloysia might be able to make some polite inquiries. Yes, why not? And if all else fails, what’s to stop them from creating their own organization or guild? They already have management experience.

### 4. Fionn Glenns

Desperate to be liked, Fionn is quick to agree with any opinion held by the person he happens to be in conversation with. And for the other person, it feels genuine and not automatic “yesmanship.” Fionn’s intelligence allows him to easily extrapolate on the perspective that he’s hearing about, and have a lot to ofer in return. However, when the conversation ends and the person goes away, Fionn returns to normal— whatever that is. No one may ever know because Fionn is loath to give any opinion about anything extemporaneously. What is clear though is that if a second person engages him in a conversation with a diametrically-opposite opinion, Fionn will instantly shift his own stance to bolster the opinion of the new person. So... it seems like he could be trusted to support you... so long as he never speaks to anyone else?
