### 5. Levina Debellator

Younger sister of Ignifera (page 84) and Gelàre Debellator (page 71), she has always felt like an outsider in the family due to the closeness of the older twins. Because of their respective interests in arcane spells they imagined themselves as a secret super-duo: “Fire & Ice.” At first it was just a silly game they played at home, but as they got older they became a lot more enamoured with the idea, and it spread to school. People thought it was cute and clever, and at its height there was an undercurrent of haters who envied their closeness and their popularity. She knows, because she was one of them. She kept hoping that there might be a place in their partnership for her someday—“Fire, Ice & Lightning” maybe? It doesn’t sound as good. But that doesn’t seem like it will ever happen now. They seem distant now; even strained. She never wanted this to happen and now feels guilty for ever having been jealous.

### 6. Colla Ó Caisidei

Trust came slowly to Colla, who unlike his brother Sòlas Ó Caisidei (page 20) has learned to read people better than his father ever did. In retrospect, it seems obvious that the more desperate people are, the less likely it is they can or will honour an agreement. In his life, he never loans anything he wouldn’t be prepared to part with, gives people one chance to make it right when they disappoint him, and only puts distance between himself and others when they’ve made it clear he’s only important when they need something. He’s weighing his feelings for his brother as of late, who seems to forget he exists for long stretches, but then has urgent need for assistance when he becomes upset or anxious. There must be some way to break him out of the shell he’s in.

### 7. Nerthus Imbrex

A notorious collector of things lost, most days of the week Nerthus will surely have picked up something dropped, forgotten or misplaced and deposited it on a shelf in her home. She’s not a thief; just the opposite, in fact. Crime being what it is these days, the likelihood of thieves absconding with your item before you return for it is high. Lost something? Drop by and describe what you mislaid. No, you may not enter. There’s been a problem with thief shoplifters in the past. Lost pet? She has those, too. Lost child? What kind of parent are you? You might ask Eileanóra Yorke (page 18). When Nerthus finds children, they’re delivered to her.

### 8. Dagfinn Frode

If Dagfinn was a book, he’d be an open one. He loves books, and the idea of becoming one after he passes away would tickle him much more than the alternative of mouldering underground. He regards his library like a shelf of old friends. He greets them cheerily by name when reaching for them from the shelf—and smells them when he opens them. If that seems repellent, you’re another of the many people he’s met in his life that shunned him for his idiosyncrasies rather than cherished him for his remarkable gifts. He is brilliant but always willing to share what he knows. Surprising, given how poorly he’s been treated. He very much wants to believe in people, but it’s his books that have proven more trustworthy.

### Thieves (1d8)

### 1. Eoforwine Del Bosques

When the expectations put upon you are high and endless, and there doesn’t seem to be any connection between how well you do and how well you are treated, is there any point in playing by the rules anymore? Eoforwine doesn’t think so. His sister Hilda (page 21) understands better than anyone what drove him into the thieves trade. He has no malice for others, but he wants results in return for his efort. When you try to influence people to give you what you want, your best attempts often yield nothing. However, when you just take what you want—you get it. No wondering, no disappointment. If he had any of his sister’s magical abilities, he could surely raise his earnings to a new level. Perhaps he can open her mind to thinking of new ways to use her magic for real benefit.

### 3. Barra O'Bernei

After tiring of the routine of being a daytime pickpocket fleecing people in the marketplace, Barra tried his luck as a night prowler. It was then that he discovered that his true love of the trade was not merely the coin and the valuables he pilfered—but the terror he evoked. He prefers to break into peoples’ homes during the night, quietly gathering items before waking up the victim to demand to know where their secret treasures are hidden. If no suitable homes are to be found, he’ll stalk women walking alone, gagging them with his filthy handkerchief. He is a coward, making most of his living from widows. He will never attack or enter a domicile where a man might be present.

### 4. Lachaidh Magees

Lachaidh is quite a talented thief, but he’s either insecure or just enjoys playing mind games with others. Maybe both. He is quick to find fault in others: their personality, their performance, their apparent loyalty to the guild—anything that might cast a critical eye on a competitor. His own professed loyalty notwithstanding, that is how he looks upon other guild members. Entirely hypocritical, he shamelessly holds other people to higher standards than himself. Should they point this out, he accuses them of deflecting. “That you’re lashing out at me proves my point, I think.” If he ends up dead, no one would be surprised. They’d just be curious which of his numerous enemies was the one to finally do it.

### 6. Kaelyn Alveye

Kaelyn was raised in an orphanage, and was never given any information about how it was she came to be there. When she was seven, a nice old woman arrived to take her away to a beautiful and spacious home. There she was given a blanket to sleep on in the back of the broom closet that was her personal quarters. For the next 8 years she lived as a servant there, cooking and cleaning, and sleeping from the time that the bell stopped ringing at night until it resumed again in the morning. One day, the bell hadn’t rung long after sunrise. When she went to the woman’s room she found her still in bed, dead and cold. Fearing that she would be blamed and punished, she gathered clothes, money, one large kitchen knife and a cleaver, and she set of wandering. Keeping to the busy roads, she found an Inn to hire her as a sous-chef and occasional waitress. The pay is poor but she was given a full room, and she overhears a lot of information casually shared during conversations. When her room door was broken down in the middle of the night, she scrambled to avoid the clutches of a drunken man intent on doing her harm. With little option, she sunk the knife into his chest. Hearing the racket, her employer bolted into the room. “Finally got what he deserved,” she said. “Empty his pockets, but leave a copper or two. The rest is yours.” Kaelyn’s job duties increased that day, though her responsibilities are strictly in-house, selling information and dealing with problem patrons.

### 8. Leviticus Puck

Leviticus is not a thief, just a trader. The problem is that it’s a rather one-sided agreement. He sees; he covets; he exchanges. He never takes anything he doesn’t already have. He never leaves people with nothing. What he does is provide himself with an upgrade—newer for older, fancier for plainer, and sometimes operational for broken. He’s quite smitten with his take on thievery and can often be seen quafing an ale with his good friend Sindri Färberg (page 61), asking, “So, how was your day then?” and laughing before changing the subject.

Dedicated servant of the Temple of Now, he’s often to be found proselytizing in marketplaces, at town fairs, and sometimes in front of the temples of other faiths. ### 2. Wilton Waters

“You don’t need to have the initials W.W. to work here, but it helps!” was Wilton’s special joke with his friend and colleague Whitney Worthame (page 108), both of the Temple of Worms. It was actually starting to grate on her just a little, to be honest, when he abruptly stopped. He’s still recognizably his same-old self, quiet and cheerful but also spontaneously exuberant—but only about half of the time now. The rest of the time he seems to be in a daze. He forgets names and details and sometimes isn’t always sure of what’s just happened around him. Whitney is wondering if it’s health-related but doesn’t know how to broach the subject without ofending him, but there are other possible causes as well. In this case, he is a host of the Vitni. Like Sylva Demetra (page 26) his conscious experiences are being observed by a non-corporeal being inhabiting his body. Unlike her, the frequent overrides on his behaviour are creating conflicts for control, which manifest themselves in observable episodes of confusion. And they are getting longer. Will they lengthen to the point where Wilton essentially becomes catatonic, or will the Vitni find someone else to inhabit? It may be possible to drive the Vitni away if two Clerics cast Dispel Good and Dispel Evil on him simultaneously.

### 3. Llewellyn Haige

If you call Llewellyn superstitious, he’ll deny it. He honestly doesn’t believe he is. He’s just been traumatized by some weird coincidences and he’s mistaken it for causality. Mirrors, for example, are particularly frightening to him. Pull out a mirror to shave with and he will yelp, double over, and slink away as quickly as he can hobble. You might suspect he was a vampire if he wasn’t such an ardent sun-worshipper. Not literally; he loves to wake up early and watch the sun rise. But he believes mirrors function as a door for the undead, and putting two mirrors facing each other opens a passage for an Infernal. Or perhaps evil is just attracted by men shaving? Is shaving a ritual to summon evil? Is this a good time to mention that Llewellyn is terrified of undead and infernals? It’s an odd characteristic for a Cleric, but his mother told him the only way he would ever conquer his fears is by facing them head on. Bad advice, but she certainly meant well by it.

### 4. Hildred Gladwyne

Noticeably older than her peers, Hildred is starting her life over again—diferently this time, she hopes. Hating everything about who she was in her former life but unable to leave it behind, Hildred sought the intervention of Ulfhrafn of Eysteinn, master of magic afecting the memory. He cast a spell of his own design—tabula rasa— to wipe away all traces of her past self. All she began with was her literacy skills, and a note written to herself which she did not recognize. After assurances from Ulfhrafn, who prompted her to recopy her own note so she could see that the handwriting matched, she joined the Temple of the Invisible, as the note instructed her. Having reached nearly the same level of power in less than half the time, the Temple’s ruling council is wondering whether to send her out into the community to find new recruits. They see her potential, but also the risk that even under her new name she might be noticed by her former associates and lured back into her old life.

### 8. Rovena Wyatte

Rovena is wound a little too tightly to begin with, and when she gets nervous she talks to relieve her anxiety—except it doesn't work. It accelerates into a stream-of-consciousness barrage of loosely connected words. Sometimes it’s a shopping list; sometimes it’s her cats’ names in alphabetical order; sometimes it’s oversharing personal topics you wish you could “unhear.” She knows she has a problem and has tried to get a grip on it. Lately it’s just ended up punctuating the stream with hiccups. It’s not good for spellcasting; it’s even worse for avoiding wandering monsters. Please do not invite her on your adventure, unless you thrive on that sort of unpredictability.

### 2. Asbjorn Stigandr

Asbjorn Stigandr—"godly bear wanderer" in his native tongue—has been a nomad since his teens. When he became uncomfortable with the life that his mother was planning for him one step at a time, he fled south on foot, singing and playing his lute to distract himself from the cold. When he reached a second civilization, he took a room in a slum-house and found work as a mercenary and is still there. He is seeking either a partner or a party with complementary skills who might join him—or whom he might join—to continue adventuring. Someone with thief or magic skills would be ideal, but anyone would be better than the loneliness of no one.

### 3. Afton Airaldii

Courageous, kind, and empathetic, Afton is called a “new age fighter” by people trying to talk kindly about him. Most people do, even the older generation of warriors who squirm on their bar stools at his curious mix of terrifying brutality and warm sensitivity. When he isn’t butchering monsters he likes to unwind by singing and painting. He is chaste, and a romantic at heart. He envisions a future where he owns a beautiful cottage by a lake, and near the forest. By day he will comb the woods, killing goblins and any others who might do harm. By night he will eat roasted meat by the fireside and talk with his loving wife about their dreams for the future. If you have a problem with any of that, why not take it up with him yourself?

### 4. Ingvar Skuld

Ingvar has always been curious about people— especially Humans. What makes them who they are? What are the individual diferences that make a person unique from others? He’s spent many years in his workshop trying to find out, and the declining social and economic conditions have given him plenty to work with. There’s always much more out on the street than the man with the cart has room to collect. Unfortunately, he’s still no closer to an answer, and now he is starting to run out of room. Time to bury some of his failed experiments to free up some more space. He’s already learned that the cart man doesn’t accept partials, and putting them back together has never been as successful as taking them apart.

### 5. Audhild Hallbjorn

Audhild is aflicted with a very rare condition, pogonophobia—the fear of beards. As a Dwarf, this is particularly tragic given that nearly all males—and some of the females—have a proud display of facial hair. Upon encountering such a person she goes berserk. Rolling a d10, a 1-3 indicates that she flees the area in a random direction—roll 1d10 again and use the “Missiles That Miss” rule to determine where. On a 4-6, she will scream uncontrollably until the person leaves or her voice gives out. On a 7-9 she will attack immediately, swinging once, then running. On a 10, roll twice (ignoring 10s) and combine the two reactions in a way that makes sense.

### 7. Greysen Adaire

Greysen once tried to be part of a team, but didn’t have the right mindset. In all things, he means to win—at any cost. Fairness is a noble quality, but what works for pricing produce and grain in the marketplace doesn’t apply where lives are at stake—especially his. As a fighter with only moderate abilities, he would be long dead had he not used his experience and charm to get a leg up on colleagues and competition. Unless he needs you alive, never trust him with your back. If only one of you gets to survive today, it won’t be you. His cousin, Cesan Finnegani (page 53), is captain of the guard and feeds him inside information and also protects him from people he’s angered. In return, Greysen keeps an eye on his back and does the odd job when Cesan is unhappy with the wheel of justice.

### 3. Eardwulf Harlowe

An unfortunate miscast of polymorph self—a spell higher than his ability to cast it—has left Eardwulf permanently and partially transformed. He is somewhat recognizable as an Elf, but also has physical characteristics of a black bear. Additionally, his normal speech is periodically interrupted by growls, groans, grumbles, and shrieks and he has recently started packing on weight in anticipation of winter hibernation. The Temple of Ursus has agreed to allow him the use of their vegetable shed for his nap, provided that he agrees not to snack on them prior to his sleep. In the interim, he is very embarrassed by his appearance and uses his spells to charm, hide, or divert attention away. He hopes his incantations aren’t randomly punctuated by bear talk. Who knows what might happen?

### 4. Gelàre Debellator (“Ice”)

Older brother of Levina (page 58) and non-identical twin of Ignifera Debellator (page 84), he is half of the self-styled duo “Fire & Ice.” It might sound stupid to some people, but it was just the sort of joyous invention that kids create when they’re happy—and he felt so bonded to her. They couldn’t actually cast any spells back then, it was just imagination. But somehow, with the passing time it evolved into an unspoken plan between them. They should—they must— become these people someday. And the plan seemed to be working. Side by side, their mastery of real spells put them on the path from kids playing “pretend” to wizards forming the core of a future adventuring party. But then she pulled away from him. Not intentionally—or not at first, at least. Her powers were still growing while he seemed to falter. She moved up to the next level; he still hasn’t made it. But the worst thing was when she mastered the spell that was supposed to be his spell: ice storm. That hurt. It felt like betrayal; like mocking. He can’t look at her the same way anymore. She acts like everything is fine. He does his best not to recoil from her.

### 6. Muirgen Ennisi

As rigid and unforgiving as plate mail may be, it’s wildly elastic when compared to Muirgen. No shenanigans, no hootenanny, no nonsense—no way, no how. And if you think that buying him an ale at a pub might wrestle the grimace on his face into a smile, think again. More relentlessly optimistic people than you have tried and failed. ### 8. Saturnus Stolo

Since he was a young person, Saturnus has felt like there was a giant void within him; like he wasn’t whole. His mother took him to the Healer to be checked; everything was fine. But the feeling never went away. He told his mother he loved her. He didn’t know what it meant, but he understood it was something she wanted to hear, so he said it. Now much older, he still doesn’t really understand it, or many of the other rituals he’s had to learn in order to function better alongside other people. He’s watched people embrace and cry, scream and fight, joke and laugh. None of it resonates. It all goes into his very long book of observations. He has chapters for individuals that he meets, and a separate section for general traits that seem to apply to everyone. It’s been a good resource. His relationships are better so long as he keeps his notes up to date. It’s also why he likes spells and magical research. If you’re a good student, you can always make adjustments to improve the outcome of your experiments, even if you’re not always certain why the changes help. It’s all about getting satisfactory results.

### Thieves (1d8)

### 1. Rollo Kara

Rollo Kara hides in plain sight. Anywhere there is a city march, consciousness-raising event, or other public demonstration—especially protests —Rollo is there. His fellow attendees have come to regard him as a social activist. In truth, anywhere people are gathered in great numbers, voices are loud, and emotions are high is a bonanza for a pickpocket. Jumping, shouting, climbing, jostling, charging—it’s just the sort of chaos where collision with others is expected and accepted. When people find themselves back at home without their coin purse they assume they must have dropped it on the ground during the commotion. Genius!

### 3. Egil Gerd

In a just and fair existence there would have been some way for Egil to have been a wizard. It was certainly his interest, if not his destiny. That such things are simply impossible makes no sense to him. So he instead turned his intellect and swiftness to thieving, with a particular interest in ambidextrous fighting and trap setting. He’s worked steadily as a mercenary since he came to the area, and he’s almost always worked alone—though not by choice. There have been times here and there where he worked with one or more specialists on a job, but nothing that begged continuing. Perhaps the universe just intends for him to remain solitary.

### 2. Wolfgang Eks

Anyone not knowing Wolfgang very well might take him for a cheapskate. He’s unquestionably protective of his money and property, but for good reason. Owing to a feud that ended before he was ever born, he was raised in a hermitage far flung from his parents’ home community. With no one to trade with, they needed to be completely self-suficient, but the land wasn’t fertile and the wildlife were sparse. Consequently he grew up in extreme poverty and only narrowly avoided an early death from undernourishment and starvation. When he was old enough to set of alone, he did, but with nowhere to go, life wasn’t appreciably better. Thievery started as a means of survival and continued because he didn’t know what talents he might have. After all, most of his life has been devoted to staying alive.

### 4. Sieglinde Bergfalks

“Siggie” is a self-styled artist of marginal talent whose main source of income is depleting the funds of whichever man or woman she happens to be with at the time. She is an expert in spotting successful but insecure people, dazzling them, and then abruptly withdrawing before returning again. This creates an intense need on the part of her prey to try to keep her satisfied all the time, for fear she will abandon them again. Her art sales are not a clever front—she actually believes she’s a genius—but it’s ultimately the savings of her victims that keeps her afloat. She must understand this; at any given time she is actively flirting with other prospects in order to have options should the cofers empty.

### 5. Ulf Kjellfrid

When they came to arrest him, Ulf surrendered quietly. He was a thief, but not a liar. He would not sell out his associates, but would instead confess his crime and accept imprisonment. Shackled and with a burlap bag on his head, he endured the bumpy ride to what he thought was prison. Instead, a dark room. Four hooded figures lurked in the corners while two more stood behind him with a wand and a weapon pointing menacingly. “So it’s death,” Ulf said. “I will not protest. I knew it would come.” “Not today and not from me,” said Leofsige Alvine (page 114) lowering his cowl. Ulf was given a choice: become a junior member of a covert group of overseers—The Octad—and gather intelligence on 4 persons of interest, or… return to life as he knew it. That was his last day as an independent contractor.

### 6. Gamelin Ross

Once a promising thief, Gamelin got lazy. With a wife at home to make sure there was always hot food and clean bedding, he decided to steal just enough to scrape by, and spend most of his time planted in a chair in the pub. “Aye, keepin watch,” he told himself, though he ended up carousing with any new face that might have been a potential prospect. After a while he was such a fixture it was impossible to imagine him having time to be a thief, and eventually, there wasn’t. His wife left him when she finally tired of his late nights and pitiful excuses. He discovered this when he came home and found his things packed; it was her house. So, back at the pub he presented himself to lonely Murreall, who was more than happy to marry him, put a roof over his head, tend to all his needs, and allow him to sit at the pub and make wild brags and empty promises to adventurers in search of a cunning thief. Hopefully they wise up to him quicker than his first wife did.

### 7. Ursus Nasica (“The Beggarman”)

For the non-magical, the secret to invisibility is being a person that no one wants to see. Ursus drapes himself in the garb of a beggar and positions himself in crowded areas where he is likely to see and hear a lot. Many fail to notice him at all; others do and give him a wide berth. Everyone is happy that he never actually begs from them, so no one reports him to the town guards. At the end of the day he sells what he’s witnessed to people who want to know, but not before removing his costume. Dressed normally, he’s really rather charming (CHA 15).

Some kids are exposed to the trade early. An orphan, Tycho was adopted by two thieves posing as a married couple and then sold at a slave auction for a profit. ## Seventh-Level Characters

### Clerics (1d8)

### 1. Sigurd Birger

Sigurd, devoted servant of the Temple of the Sun, woke up this morning and realized his destiny— the saviour of all living-kind. The Deity did not speak it to him in words; he rose from his bed infused with the truth. It will need to be done with utmost secrecy. Surely jealousy would upend the table of the council as they squabble about which of them is most deserving of this special distinction. No—his mission is to restore the sect to its rightful power and primacy, not to foment chaos from within. He must leave immediately, take the message on the road, never to return until the work is done, a wooden crate for his pulpit.

### 2. Lucas Sabinus

Lucas is the last prophet and sole resident of the building now known as the Temple of the Dire Wolf. Any other “members” that are seen praying or welcoming visitors at the entrance are actually “volunteers” whose lives Lucas has agreed to spare—along with their families—from his blood thirst. Over a hundred and fifty years ago, all former members of the ruling council left to found the Temple of Eternal Life. Lucas, a thenjunior member of the council, was chosen by the Deities to assume the role of Chair. Angered, the senior members all left; he remained. Out of fear, the congregation soon followed. It fell to Lucas to try to rebuild the entire membership, but no one dared join him. He died there as an elderly man, prostrated in supplication before the altar. A red circle of light surrounded him, and by the following morning his fallen body had changed to the form of a dire wolf curled and asleep. He remained there for a hundred years. During that time, anyone who entered the temple and crossed the circle, vaporized. When he finally stirred from his rest, Lucas understood what he was—a shape-changed vampire. He then commandeered the participation of families and gave word to spread the news that it was safe again to enter the temple. The Deities infused any who prayed there with clerical powers— provided that they showed interest in killing the undead. In time, when suficient numbers exist, the new adherents will receive the order from the Deities—to descend upon the Temple of Eternal Life and destroy it.

### 3. Posy Hayes

Being raised from the dead has had a profound efect on Posy. Her faith, never wavering, has only become stronger since her return. She’s been less able to cope with having several years of her knowledge and experiences lost as a result of her death. It’s a wound she still feels, along with the physical scars she’s been left with as a reminder of the ordeal. She doesn’t wish that she’d have died permanently, but learning to live again is more dificult than she could have ever imagined. She still has faint impressions of the fatal blow and it replays endlessly some days. The idea of ever venturing out of the temple again seems impossible.

### 8. Evadne Cento

Evadne’s name means “good and holy” and she wears it well. Selfless and intuitive, she thinks of others first and knows how to reach them emotionally. She is known as someone who shoulders great responsibility and gets things done. While strong and independent, she understands that unity comes from working together. She has a talent for inspiring and motivating others into collective action. When her friend and frequent collaborator Mallory Freemane (page 92) invited her over for tea one day it came as a shock to see four other guests already seated before her. Leofsige Alvine (page 114) then stood, introduced himself and asked Evadne to swear on her faith to not disclose the ofer he was about to make to her. She readily accepted a place as the sixth member of the covert group of overseers known as The Octad.

### Fighters (1d8)

### 1. Sverre Agnar

For the longest time, Sverre thought of nothing except how he could win the love and admiration of his father. He understands how he must have once seemed a bitter disappointment. He was lazy and ignorant and had a lot of growing up to do. But then it was like a lantern had been lit in his mind. He pursued excellence and mastery in a way that he never had the will to before. He discovered potential he wasn’t aware he had, and developed abilities that would shock anyone who knew him as a young lad. But it’s too late. There’s seemingly nothing he can accomplish that makes him any more than the lazy ne’er-do-well that he once was. Then came the realization that his siblings, who had better beginnings but considerably less success, still fill his father’s chest with pride. It’s maddening. He’s still not over it. There’s nothing to be done except push his father out of his mind and his heart for good. And he won’t be at his graveside when the old man is finally laid to rest.

### 5. Thora Gunhild

When she first started training as a fighter, one of Thora’s instructors criticized her for her solitary tendencies. “Why aren’t you spending your downtime socializing with your group?” he said. “High morale saves lives.” And so she made a considerable efort to hang out, talk, and laugh with others. It was fine, but also exhausting. When she found a slot in a full-time adventuring group it was necessary to do it all again; invest time and efort into getting to know the people she’d be depending on. Now it’s done, but it seems like an inescapable expectation. Whenever she goes out to the forest alone to lean against a tree and listen to the Creator’s message on the breath of the wind, inevitably she’ll be tracked and interrupted: “Whatcha doin’?” None of them seem to understand how important it is for her to disengage and decompress. They think there’s something wrong. Nothing is wrong. She needs solitude.

### 6. Fionnghuala Duanei

There was a time in her life when she felt small and helpless. When she closes her eyes, it doesn’t feel that long ago. Being a fighter makes her feel strong, but it’s hard for her to control. And it’s hard to explain. The feelings are clear in her head but she doesn’t have the words to say them. Right now, she’s still trying to make sense of them. Hitting feels good. Anger feels good; but it’s blinding. It becomes hard to know when to stop. Not everyone is deserving of it in equal amounts, but knowing when to stop or being able to seems impossible. And there’s something satisfying in cruelty; for a moment at least. The shame comes quickly, but never lingers long enough to prevent the same sequence all over again. She feels like a bad person doing bad things, and that she must have deserved the bad things that once happened to her. It’s so confusing, like a tornado of thoughts swirling in her head. She will never have the right words.

### 7. Dane Kerr

Upon the completion of a successful adventure, Dane returned to an empty home. A neighbour broke the terrible news: his wife and children had been taken in the middle of the night about a month before. The village looked but couldn’t find them; no one knew how to reach Dane. For the next 3 years, he searched. He walked every road, talked to every stranger, and paid thieves for information. Nothing. So he returned to the road. He worked alone for a while before joining a party, then quit to return to life as a mercenary. He can’t feel close to people anymore. He’s tired of people’s concerns and questions. No one could understand this emptiness. Killing was the only thing that filled the void, but it was always over too soon. Then he realized that he appreciated the distraction of pain. He sold his shield and began taking on more dangerous jobs. He hurts, but it’s a welcome diversion for his attention. When the pain fades, the void is there again. Time to work.

### 3. Ignifera Debellator (“Fire”)

Older sister of Levina (page 58) and non-identical twin of Gelàre Debellator (page 71), she is half of the self-styled duo “Fire & Ice.” Ignifera wishes she could go back a year ago to how things used to be with her brother. She doesn’t understand how, but at some point her studies became so much more important than playing games. “Fire & Ice” was fun in its time, but then it began to feel like a joke that wasn’t funny anymore. Objectively, one can know how much something used to make you laugh, but also realize it just doesn’t afect you the way it once did. She thought Gelàre felt the same, or would come to, but he never seemed to let go of the fantasy. What wizard gets by in the world using their one famous spell? Out in the field, that sort of predictability gets you killed. She feels bad for not noticing how poorly he was doing in his studies and not ofering to help. She only had good intentions when she learned his “signature spell” and also that of her younger sister. She hoped to increase her abilities and become a potential tutor to them. ### 5. Themistocles Spinther

Eldest brother to Achaicus Spinther (page 13) the information magnate, Themi is said to have taught his six younger brothers everything he ever learned as he progressed upward in the magical hierarchy. Gregarious and charismatic, he demonstrates a nearly endless interest in people, but is rather modest about his own activities and achievements. It’s a family trait. The other brothers, while successful in their own right, chose lives away from the public eye. They all meet regularly and remain incredibly close. All are also perpetually aware of Achaicus’ special status, but there is too great a sense of family pride for there to be any jealousy. Ack’s natural birthright is to eclipse everyone in the family, if not most living men (and never call him Ack, except in private). The two publicly famous brothers deny any involvement with the Coalition for Magical Cooperation. Strange, given that Themi is a wizard and Achaicus is in the clergy. They seem ideally suited to pass information between the magical lines. Of course, they do— but in secret. Unbeknownst to his brother, Themi is also a shadow member of a covert group of overseers—The Octad—led by his good friend Leofsige Alvine (page 114). Themi regrets the deception, but feels that Ack is already endangered enough by secret involvement in one organization, let alone a second one where the risk is significantly greater. At all times he is under the watchful eye of Maida Downere (page 87), unless Themi has dispatched her to watch over his brother.

### 6. Valda Duerrg

Power hungry to the last, Valda rules from the castle of her happily departed husband. Her subjects pay her taxes and homage. No one dares challenge her, let alone speak of the treachery that brought her to rule. Paschalis Sacerdos (page 22) remembers, but also remains silent and distant. He was still a boy when he watched his parents’ heads tumble into a basket. Valda pays him no mind. Many children she knew from that time drank themselves to an early grave, starved, or froze to death sleeping in ditches. Why would he have fared any better? Still, she is well aware that she is hated and prepares accordingly.

### 7. Sionn Magees

Sionn feels like he’s going through the motions, waiting to become the wizard he wants to be. His real interest is death magic and he’s already begun conducting magical research in that area. He pays a thief to “borrow” and return relevant books from the secret library of the Temple of Eternal Life. So far, no one seems to have noticed. Too poor to construct a tower, he’s converted his parents’ cellar into a passable workshop. They were not in favour of the idea and subsequently became his first test subjects. They are no longer alive, but also not functioning as undead in the way he had hoped. He needs to do more reading and it may be necessary to acquire other subjects.

### Thieves (1d8)

### 1. Slade Whittlee

Slade has always been good at playing the long game. If there was a fortune to be made playing chess, he might have the mind for it. Instead, he chose a life as an investment broker. Presenting himself as the middleman between budding investors and businesses seeking capital, Slade sells fictitious opportunities with the zeal of a preacher. Money comes in, a few early investors receive dividends back, and then more investors give him money which he is happy to take. There are even a few of the early investors that refuse the dividend, and ask for it to be reinvested. Chumps. In the end, his rented ofice is emptied, lives are ruined, and Slade moves several towns away to quietly start up again.

### 5. Grayson Wescotte

Enamoured with his own mystique, it’s a miracle that he isn’t doing time. Grayson is a very good thief, but also a self-aggrandizing showboat. He wears rings on eight fingers. He dresses in expensive and flashy clothing that fits much too tightly, and was clearly designed with much younger people in mind. He brags about how it’s a crime that he shamelessly steals the way that he does. No one pays him any mind. First, he comes of as a pathetic, middle-aged twit. Second, people assume that he’s referring to his shop, which actually serves as the front for selling all the things that he steals. As a Merchant-Thief, he’s careful with his inventory. Only common items make it to the shelves and display cases. Unique items are sold or traded with other Merchant-Thieves that are part of his guild’s network. On some rare occasions he has been approached by people he’s stolen from in order to see about getting a replacement. In a few cases he’s “miraculously” been able to furnish one—after extensive polishing and refurbishment to ensure it looks better and newer than it did when stolen. Clearly he’s not as stupid as he seems.

## Eighth-Level Characters

### Clerics (1d8)

### 1. Rodachan Whaleni

Minister to the Fallen and devoted brother of the Temple of the Invisible, Rodachan’s mission is to reach out to the very worst sinners and convince them to disappear from the lives they have, and become someone better. He attracts much ridicule and occasional violence from those who are proud of the evil they’ve done—though this has decreased somewhat since the town guards realized he was a magnet for people they wanted to arrest. An ex-thief of no reputation, he was himself saved from these same streets by an elder mentor now long since passed. As a sanctuary not open to the public, those who enter the Temple leave their clothes and any other material possessions at the entrance for donation to the poor. Inside, they receive an old robe, a new name, and a chance to reinvent themselves—provided they are open to parting with their non-material possessions. Many do, and disappear inside the sanctuary for years, or permanently. The very best are allowed out on day passes, like Rodachan.

### 2. Zephyrinus Rutilus

Resigned to accept that humanity’s fate is sealed, Zeph works as a historian in the metaphysical cartography department of the Temple of the Abyss. His job is to research and maintain a complete record of events pertaining to the imminent extinction of all life. In part, this includes historical examples of declining morality in society, major scandals, wars, plagues, astrological phenomena, weather, and any other tidbits that are arguably indicative of an approaching apocalypse. They may be helpless to stop it, but at least there will be an accurate record. They haven’t given much thought as to who might be left alive to read it, but then, there isn’t the time.

### 3. Mungan Yorke

Mungan is under considerable stress. He’s always felt a close connection with his Deity; not just while at prayer, but everywhere. Lately though, she has been entering his dreams. She is greatly concerned that the scriptures, while never properly understood, need to be read again and taught with the meanings she intended. Mungan has been chosen as her prophet. Lacking the seniority and council membership to have any input into the current practices of the Temple, he is terrified. His enactment of her proclamation will, in the very least, get him excommunicated because of the threat he poses to the establishment. But more likely he would be executed as a heretic. What to do? She’s assured him his powers are safe, whatever the Temple may say to the contrary. He could always strike out and start his own temple as some older and wiser clerics have done... but would they leave him to it?

### 4. Dahlia Ware

No one remembers what Dahlia looked like. It might have been a good idea for someone to paint a portrait of her remembered appearance before her body was destroyed, but any who might have been able to have long since passed on. Dahlia perished in the service of her Deity under terrible conditions. Abducted and tortured, her body was never released, and the head was placed in an iron box atop a castle tower where it remained under guard for most of the winter, until it was recovered by mercenary specialists. Her peers rushed her back to the Temple of the Impure where her brain was preserved in a reinforced crystal cylinder. By the Deity’s will she is able to detect individual presences (including those who are invisible), converse, answer questions, and cast spells. She can detect evil or detect magic in her presence at will and is immune to all scrying efects such as clairvoyance and mind reading.

### 5. Brìghde Jewelle

Brìghde built her home at the hub of a circle of outlying communities. She is not afiliated with any temple. She believes the Creator is everywhere, in all substances and in all beings. Her home is open to any local residents or passing travellers in need of help, but she’s also known to make home visits. She refuses no one; not even suspected criminals, and she has never been robbed. Unknown to her, there exist unwritten directives within the regional thieves guilds that she is not to be harmed—ever. She does not request coin for her services, but it is gratefully accepted. The villages and towns also help see to her needs, bringing her seasonal produce, firewood and textiles in gratitude.

### 6. Mallory Freemane

Mallory’s mother is probably the most Creator-fearing person she’d ever known—at least before befriending Averil Warwicke (page 95). At the time of her pregnancy, Hailie Freemane was concerned by what she feared were the initial signs of an approaching apocalypse. Mallory, which means “Ill-Omened” was a name meant to impart to her daughter the importance of vigilance. Mallory joined the clergy to be well placed to both watch the world for signs of evil, and be in position to do something if the signs persisted. So, when she was approached by Leofsige Alvine (page 114) and heard what he had observed, she knew they needed to work together in secret to mount a defence. They co-founded The Octad—a covert group of overseers that chose its name as a response to The Tetrad, the group it now watches. It has been some time since she heard that her brother Conrad (page 52) had left the Temple of Thorns. What is he up to?

### Fighters (1d8)

### 1. Knox Burnhame

Knox has gotten older and yet has still failed to grow. It’s true that all men remember their younger, wilder, stupider years with some fondness, and to some degree a bit of that immaturity never entirely leaves. Knox’s inner child became a teenager and never moved out. He works sporadically, bathes occasionally, and sleeps and lounges excessively. He’s charming enough to get meals paid for and a place to sleep most of the time, and for the remaining times there are haylofts and free-range poultry. He’ll work if he’s desperate and the money is good. Pay him daily at the end of the day, no matter what story he tells you. Any coin provided in advance will disappear, along with your expectations.

### 2. Tomas Cockes

Tomas’ father’s reputation as “the town drunk” caused him no end of embarrassment as a youth, but what came home every evening was worse. When he was old enough, he fled the family home and took up whatever jobs he fancied and drifted in and out of relationships, treating women like livestock. At one father’s insistence, he married the girl rather than face the consequences, but he was unhappy. His father-in-law used connections and got him a plum job as a manager, but he was unhappy. Several children were born, but he was unhappy. The only things that seem to fulfill him are fighting, money, and fame. Tomas fights, then spends on himself extravagantly. He wants to be seen about town, well-dressed and looking successful, rubbing shoulders and patting backs with people he desperately wants to impress. He fights again; he wins; he crows his victory loudly and proudly. Then he goes to the pub and drinks because he doesn’t know what else to do. You might see him there. He’ll gladly work for you. He’s anxious to get out on the road again because he doesn’t want to be at home with his wife and children. He never wanted it.

### 4. Gage “Sirius” Wickhame

Called “Sirius” by his close friends for his tendency to stargaze, Gage is struggling day-to-day with depression. Stargazing simultaneously fills him with a sense of wonder and of his own insignificance. He’s seemingly unaware of his own value. On better days he is full of charm and wit, and on any day he is a better fighter than most. But on the very worst days he wonders why he was brought into a world by those that clearly never wanted him there. If it’s even an answerable question, it’s not one he’d feel better hearing. It’s dificult to be his friend during these times. The way he tortures himself feels cyclical and infinite. He doesn’t need an argument, or even an answer. He needs a hug and some reassuring truth—that his life has bettered the lives he’s touched, and that his absence would leave a hole in that universe.

### 5. Averil Warwicke

St. Averil is what she’s called by others. Some intend it sincerely as a compliment and others as a pejorative. Helpful, kind, truthful, chaste, Creator-fearing, law abiding—she is so obviously selfless and pure that it grates on the nerves of the morally ambiguous. So it was that she came to the attention of Mallory Freemane (page 92), who made Averil many cups of tea and asked many more questions of her, before agreeing she was as close to a living saint as anyone she’d ever known—including her mother. At a meeting over tea with Leofsige Alvine (page 114) and Tuesday Rey (page 118), Averil gave her solemn vow to never speak of the subjects discussed that day. Then, a second vow whereby she pledged her service for life to The Octad as its fourth member. Keeping the secrecy of the organization’s existence is the closest thing to lying she’s ever done.

Like the tail of a comet, it’s easy to see Orvar’s path of destruction when he’s been angered. He flings himself forward—hard—and just keeps going. It’s in his name and his nature. A nomad born of nomads, he prefers simple living with few material possessions. Armour, weapons, a few tools, and coins for food and drink. He’s quite cheerful until “caged”—that is, holed up in an inn somewhere having meetings with shady characters. Then he becomes quite grumpy; never yelling, but making a near-constant mufled grumbling. He is also not a fan of trips to the market. Anything of any real value is pulled away from the bodies of the dead.

### 2. Leoflæd Beane

Born missing his left foot and only a partial shin, Leoflæd was nearly drowned at birth by his father. It was his mother that fought vehemently for his life, insisting that whatever the Creator had withheld from his body would be paid back tenfold in some other way. She hoped it was musical talent, paying for tutoring from a retired bard—but Leoflæd was hopelessly tone deaf. It was his discovery of cantrips to help with chores around the house that cued his mother that it was a great and powerful wizard she’d given birth to all those years ago. As an apprentice, he made incredible strides—and not just with his mind, but with his seemingly efortless charm and positiveness. He is destined for greatness. His prosthetic is of his own design, and the hollowed shin doubles as a case for wands and scrolls.

### 3. Zanna Chadwicke

Despite her attractiveness, charm, and warmth, Zanna has never been in a relationship. Her depth of intellect and wisdom, especially for her age, can be intimidating to others—and she wouldn’t waste her time with anyone who had to struggle with that. In any case, there’s been years of intense study and dificult work occupying her time in order to reach the level she’s achieved. She was starting to make peace with the fact that her career would consume her whole life, and that it was more than satisfactory to her. She never thought she would meet someone that she might fall in love with, but then came Carling Truemane (page 116), who is quite unlike anyone she’s ever met. His intellect ensnares her. The amount of time he’s spent learning and exploring the world to accumulate the knowledge he has is truly startling, and he’s an open and endless book of stories. They’ve spent nights awake just talking. No matter how much time they’re together, it never seems like enough. If that’s not the beginning of love, what is? The best thing about him: because of his long pursuit of knowledge, he’s never been in a relationship either. They could explore this new experience together.

### 7. Ailish Fylani

Ailish knows what she appears to be to others, but still isn’t sure what she actually is. She awoke one morning, a full-grown adult “Elf,” enveloped within a cocoon and standing inside the recess of a hollow tree. Tearing herself free, she fell to her knees, wriggling face down and backwards, out the open bottom of the trunk. When she emerged naked from the forest, confused and without language, mouths hung open. An elven woman covered her with a cloak and led her to what would become home. She learned to understand elvish in a month and speak it fluently in three. Common took another two weeks. She chose her name out of those listed in a family scrapbook. She chose the woman as her mother. The tree that she’d been born from had vanished from the forest, and when she described it, none had any recollection of having known of such a tree. Magic came naturally to her, like another language. Curious about her origin but cautious about revealing herself, she speaks of her birth casually and in the third person. No one’s heard of such a thing, nor is there mention in any of the books she’s yet read. Curious.

### 2. Kendrick Hawke

Kendrick may be the only thief ever to leave a place he’d just broken into due to the lack of cleanliness. Despite the efort it took, he just couldn’t bring himself to touch anything in order to search. Hands that could make a mess like this couldn’t possibly have anything of value. His own place is meticulous. You’d never believe that a criminal lived there, but it explains a lot about him and his success. He wouldn’t be caught dead in a wasteland, wilderness, dungeon, or catacomb. He’s strictly commercial and residential properties. And his adverseness to filth extends doubly to people. He won’t shake hands, won’t eat food that he didn’t cook himself, and won’t even allow others close enough where they might exhale air that he might then breathe. Needless to say he wears gloves and a mask at all times. His thievery record is similarly spotless. Leaving no trace of his entry, search, theft, and egress, there are some jobs where the thefts were never reported because the owners never realize that anything was stolen.

### 3. Selby Torbjorn

Selby now desperately wishes he (alternately, she) hadn’t ridiculed that old Cleric lady. He was feeling a bit too full of himself and lashed out at her to make himself feel an even bigger man. As if she’d read his mind, she waved the back of her hand in his direction and said, “Understand how it feels to be small.” At the time he didn’t know what she meant, but after a few months he started to notice the change. After a year he was an entire foot shorter than he once was. His bones ache; his clothes don’t fit; he can’t work. Lately, he’s been hanging out by the Temple of the Muse, because it’s nearest to the place that they passed each other. But he’s never seen her again. It must have been a coincidence because that Temple seems to be devoted to egos and idols. No one there will help him; they’re far more arrogant than he ever was.

### 4. Halle Haldor

Halle remembers well the day she woke up and decided she couldn’t live with herself anymore. She considered walking straight into the Temple of the Invisible, but then went to find Willow Averille (page 107) instead. Willow was shocked. Halle was the right hand of Eònan Donohuei, a master criminal who’d recently expanded from smuggling to slave trading. Halle’s primary role was as his protector and occasional assassin-atarms, and now she ofered her protection services to Willow and her organization. But Willow, fearing that Halle would be killed for desertion, escorted her under armed guard to the Temple of the Invisible. In the presence of many witnesses, curious to see who was going in, Halle entered the Temple and was not seen again— until the following morning. Emerging in her robe, face hidden by the shadow of the cowl, Halle walked the streets, keeping a respectable distance from her new employer, ever watchful. She repeats this each day, unaware that her former employer has retained Cyprian Ovicula (page 100) to investigate whether her entering the Temple was an act of conscience or a clever deception.

### 5. Everild Baines

Everild has recently been released after a lengthy stay in prison. A combination of inmate overcrowding and the irritating protesters assembling daily outside his walls convinced the ruler to let a handful of prisoners go free. “Are you right or left handed?” the guard asked, before chopping of his left hand without waiting for an answer. Everild has basic equipment again, which he’d stored in a cousin’s barn. Incarceration gave him a lot of time to busy his mind while his body atrophied. He’s torn between retraining as a wizard or trying to continue his old trade with his new disability. Either path might be on hold while he considers revenge against the guard who maimed him.

### 6. Ciara Ó Conailli

Elder cousin of Niamh (page 31), she might work with him if she didn’t view him as a direct competitor to getting what she believes is rightfully hers—everything. She’s just as arrogant as he, but better at concealing it. She’s noticed many successful criminals who damaged both their external reputation and inside loyalty by assuming that demonstrating fierce, erratic behaviour would make them look strong. Ciara is extraordinarily reserved. Her delicate tones and carefully weighed words are stupefying. The quieter she speaks to you, the more trouble you’re probably in. With this softly menacing approach she’s amassed a number of dedicated followers. Their next assignment may be to remove Niamh from the running before he gets the same idea about her.

### 7. Rylee Thatchere

Smart, but not nearly as smart as she (alternately, he) thinks she is, Rylee will never work in the information trade because she is constantly alienating people. While initially very charismatic, it’s a ruse. Rylee will tell anyone whatever it is they want to hear, to get whatever it is she needs out of them, then replace that person with a better one when the opportunity presents itself. Not an efective way to build a network. Perhaps a switch to livestock or commodities trading would be more suitable?

### 8. Waldenburg Bradleye

Sworn enemy of Roderick Wylie (page 101) and possibly the best thief to ever practice the trade, or so his stories would lead you to believe. No matter what tales of derring-do are shared over the firelight, Waldenburg has one to top it. He did it first, better, and faster. He travelled farther, dug deeper and climbed higher. He picked the lock with his right hand while parrying with his left. It’s hilarious until it’s stupid and you wish it would stop. Best you can do is look for another fire and hope that he doesn’t follow behind.

### 2. Jarl Fuhrmanng

Formerly of the Temple of Now, Jarl sensed a growing undercurrent of hatred forming for the non-Human members of the sect. While he considered it a betrayal, given that the early success of the Temple was directly attributable to the membership and work of non-Humans, he also realized it was inevitable. It’s what the Humans do. But he also realized that he’d learned a lot about designing, creating and managing such an organization, and marketing it efectively to grow membership. He founded the Temple of Deimos: god of terror and dread. He wasn’t even sure that Deimos still lived—not until the spells worked, anyway. But now he believes in Deimos, though not quite as much as he believes in himself. This stands an excellent chance of scooping up so many isolated, angry and disillusioned citizens, and is almost guaranteed to absorb the entire non-Human member base of the Temple of Now, when it finally boils over.

### 3. Euphemia Servilianus

Euphemia loves the sound of her own voice. How fitting it is that she would become a priestess of the Temple of the New, a clerical startup in need of research marketing. Religion is on the decline and where there is a trend downward it’s always a sign that the current providers have lost touch with the needs of the people. Euphemia hit the marketplace to find out why. The major complaint of those leaving faith behind is the amount of work involved with piety. It’s not just the good behaviour within oneself that’s exhausting, but the need to extend that efort to everyone else. Compassion, empathy, generosity —the list is endless. Who has the patience— which is another tiring virtue—to give that much to others with no expectation of receiving any of it back—another ridiculous requirement of the old scriptures. So, as a member of the Temple of the New, you need only provide a monthly tithe and your personal assurance that you’re trying your best. If you’re a happy person inside and not intentionally harming anyone else, you’re golden. Profits are up a little this month; just think of all those shiny souls!

### 4. Hjalmar Ingeborg

If leaving his community physically distanced Hjalmar from his Halfling roots, joining the Temple of Now severed any remaining similarities between who he was then, and who he is now. Leaving home began as a simple walk after an argument with his family. But then he just kept walking. The further he went, the more self- assured he felt that he was right and the less reason there was to acquiesce. Joining the clergy was an impulse. He needed food and a place to sleep. They ofered. All he had to do was listen. He did, for a few months, and then set of again. As a travelling man of the cloth he found many doors opened to him and knew then that his walk had sent him on the path he was meant to follow. He entered any door that was opened, listened to any sermon that was given, and ate any meal that was provided. When his mind and body was full, he moved on. When he reached the Temple of Now, he stopped. Here he found people frustrated with declining values in an overly permissive society. Here he heard people decry the tolerance of social movements and protests and marches—the sudden obsession with rights. Here he saw clerics who took their message to the streets, but also their maces—beating down the rebels and reprobates who were unwilling or unable to be saved from themselves. If this isn’t the solution to saving a society quickly crumbling under the weight of its wickedness, it’s the closest thing his travels have found. He is ready to die in its service if need be.

### 5. Godiva Lockshorne

As a high priestess of the Temple of New Birth, Godiva walks among the people as her doctrine prescribes—just as her Deity made her. In this place and at this time it’s not considered shocking. It’s a stark reminder of commonality, a warning against secretiveness, and an appeal for more truth and vulnerability in daily interaction. A relatively new sect, it’s gained a lot of traction by those concerned by the amount of crime and corruption festering in society—especially in cities. What isn’t certain is whether this message, presented in this format, will actually do anything to influence anyone.

### 6. Maitane Airaldii

Maitane distinguished herself early in her clerical career for her ability to work well with others without emotional entanglement. Because of this, she’s gradually been invited to to share more of her abilities with the inner circle of the temple. Now sitting at the table of elite, she’s disturbed by the amount of dysfunction. The pure-intent to do right is still there, but the council’s desire to have complete harmony has seriously afected its decision-making process. No proposition put before them is able to pass until it has been added to and deleted from until it bears only passing resemblance to the original. There’s no anger. In fact, everyone is on their best behaviour. But the need to accommodate all opinions so that the finished product reflects everyone’s viewpoint is creating some terrible policy. It could lead to a mass-exit of faithful and a collapse of the Temple at a point where it is already struggling to remain open.

### 7. Willow Averille

Friend of Blossom Averye (page 113) and a co-founding member of the famed Coalition for Magical Cooperation, she’s also the foster mother to Carlyn Donovani (page 9). Although she appears strong and confident externally, Willow often reflects on how she might have become an entirely diferent person. Clearly, she had the ingredients to become the success she is, but there was much about her as a younger woman that might have steered her wrong—especially during her teens. She was always good at thinking about things in the long term. Perhaps that’s what saved her? No—definitely not—at least not alone. She was always a disciplined and organized person, a tireless worker, and very focused on having a successful career someday. But her intense drive and early success turned her past the point of confidence and assertiveness and into arrogance and self- aggrandizement. She became insular and completely self-centred and for a time refused to have any involvement with others unless she was granted control. In the process, she drove away every friend she’d ever made. All except Blossom. Blossom persisted through the rage, until the anger fractured into tears, and eventually into realization, regret and reform. It wasn’t the magical transformation from children’s fairy tales. It took years, and she’s still not convinced that she isn’t still broken inside. She also understands that her behaviour didn’t come from nowhere, but that there’s no point in blaming. Regardless of what’s been done to you, who you become is a choice you make for yourself. Thank the Creator for Blossom. It was Blossom that convinced her that there were wrongs to be righted everywhere, and that it could be their mission to use magic to do it. She’d never felt so small in a long time, until she realized that it was her inflated sense of urgency to protect herself constantly that was gone. In being given a purpose, she’d been returned to her place.

### 8. Whitney Worthame

Elder sister of Wymond (page 83). Deeply devoted to her faith but non-traditionally open-minded and free-thinking, one might think that Whitney would be an adherent of the Temple of the New or the Temple of Now. She’s not certain that either of those are legitimate. At best, she thinks they are vanity organizations founded by power-hungry Clerics that were unable to get control of the organizations they came up in. The Temple of Worms is an ancient sect founded on simple truths. Where worms are active, the soil prospers and beauty grows. Where worms are absent, beauty withers and evil takes root. The Temple of Worms seeks to spread their numbers and leave evil with nowhere to grow unchecked. It’s an old wisdom, not as trendy or edgy as the alternatives, but not ready to disappear into the past, either. With new generations of clergy like her and Wilton Waters (page 64) — nonjudgmental, not dogmatic, more connected to society — the Temple is holding its own. And with young people like Brianag Mohani (page 38) coming in the front door, these “next-generation” temples may disappear as quickly as other local fads. Come to think of Wilton, she’s actually quite worried about the health of her friend. He’s just not himself recently.

### 2. Pádraigín O'Doylei

It’s a sad tale. The first time he fought in armed combat, Pádraigín wet himself. It’s common for first timers, he heard, but he was so embarrassed he never returned to that town again except to pack his things and move on. On to the next town, and the next, and the next, and surely the last one—but no. He had the physicality of a fighter, but not the courage. While his fighting didn’t improve he did get rather good at using his nimbleness to scoop up better equipment for himself. Halfling-sized magic armour was hard to come by, but when he finally saw it, there wasn’t a lineup for it. Now very protected, he wets himself considerably less, but still needs to change towns now and again when the “Pee Pee Pádraigín” chants start up again, inevitably.

### 3. Wulfstan Hunte

Wulfstan was conscripted into military service by a warlord at the age of 7. An army swept through his hamlet, forcibly taking all boys who looked like they could be of use. Any parents that resisted the abductions were killed. He witnessed other parents being killed by soldiers, but not his own. He was assured that they were also killed for their attempts to deceive and conceal. As part of an assessment process, Wulfstan was tested with various responsibilities including food preparation, equipment management, looting, intelligence gathering, field medical assistance, and squire duty. Eventually he graduated into a soldier, rising to oficer class through a combination of bravery, talent, and loss of leaders from ongoing casualties and occasional desertion. At 17, distressed by the duties of his role, Wulfstan collapsed after taking non-lethal damage and lay in a pile of bodies pretending to be dead. In his army there was no ceremony. Fallen soldiers were left to rot where they lay. When the Warlord departed, Wulfstan salvaged some equipment and set of to work for the next 18 months as a courier, bodyguard, guide and mercenary for hire, but he misses being part of a troop. He’s grown a beard and changed his name. While technically he was abandoned by the Warlord, he still fears recognition and discovery.

### 6. Bliktrud Dürrg

With the usual nagging ailments fighters get from physically punishing themselves day after day, Bliktrud had no reason to worry when he visited the Healer-on-Duty at the Temple of Wounds. He did begin to grow concerned when that Cleric asked his advisor for a second opinion. The news was grim. If they cut of his leg, he might live a full life. If not, he might have a year. It was devastating. He stood, turned, and walked out of the Temple without saying a word and without looking back. Even as he did it, he knew what it meant. He thought that he had a lifetime to live. Now, he has only the question of what to do with the time he has left.

### 7. Isold Gerverg

A career-long monster hunter, she started from humble beginnings killing rats in pub cellars for food and lodging, to her current trade—killing much more terrifying beasts for food and lodging. And coin—lots of coin. But it’s surprising how much she enjoys going to work when it’s someone else’s responsibility to ensure she is fed and rested. She’s enjoying road trips less and less as she gets older, so she’s now making travel arrangements the client’s responsibility. Choose carefully. If she doesn’t like what you send to pick her up, she’s not coming to fight your monster.

### 8. Marlee Denmane

Marlee has a tragic backstory. She’ll tell it to you if you really want to hear it; she’s not bothered by it anymore—but it has nothing to do with the person she’s become. She’s heard more than her fair share of moaning from drunken thieves complaining about what ruined their lives and led them to their desperate existence. What a load of road apples. It’s people like them that left her an orphan. Why would she do what was done to her, to another person? She wouldn’t, that’s what. If she did slip in that direction, it would be as a vigilante—removing people like them. But, that’s not who she is. She’s not quite invested enough in the social welfare system to volunteer any time helping one of the local do-good organizations. She’s not a saint, and the world still isn’t doing her any favours. Maybe when she doesn’t have her own problems to manage.

### Magic-Users (1d8)

### 1. Ortrud De Vend

Eldest sibling to Aloysia (page 57) and Sighilde De Vend (page 20), it feels like a long time since he gave any thought to the historical family business —general merchandise sales. He didn’t hear the calling, as much as his father might have wished it. As the first child to break away, he dove headlong into his arcane studies at the risk of being disowned, but his father loved him much more than he ever realized. Nothing Ortrud could do would be a true disappointment, so long as it was performed at a level of greatness. Rising toward the top of the wizarding class, he began to see it for what it was: a political minefield. If there is some glorious golden room where mages embrace and reflect on the good that they’ve accomplished putting their powers to work, Ortrud hasn’t found it. There’s a back room, certainly, but one filled with schemers plotting how to concentrate power into as few hands as possible. Perhaps it’s time to go back to the store, at least for a while. Taking stock of his life, it feels much more draining than taking stock on the shelves once did.

### 2. Philocrates Murena

Philocrates took notice when he saw thieves already eyeing his tower as it was still being constructed. He realized he needed to build a trap so fiendish that any thief that dared to enter would never leave. Then, upon further reflection, he thought there might need to be more than one. Security works best in layers. His first protection was darkness. Other than the single entrance, there are no portals and no windows. The only light inside is magical. There are candles, but they trigger a silent alarm. Second, there are clues at the entrance that suggest the presence of a trap, and additional clues in the foyer once the thief breaches the door. These are decoys intended to be found. Disarming them provides a soft but satisfying-sounding click, but otherwise has no efect. Third, there are many Magic Mouths strategically placed along the walls; so many that they look like an extension of the tower’s overall Gargoyle decor motif. These remain completely silent when passed by Philo or one of his designates, but scream at maximum volume when passed by an intruder. Depending on where a thief has managed to get to, one or several trap doors may open in the intruder’s proximity. Type I contains acid; Type II is filled with rats; Type III is filled with undead; Type IV is a standard spike pit that already imprisons a gelatinous cube. Variations of these also exist with an illusionary floor instead of a trap door. Philo is very pleased by the early results and would love to get more accurate statistics from the tally he’s been keeping, but there’s been a sudden loss of interest in breaking in. Still, he’s thinking of adding some bronze golems, for that classy suit-of-armour decor look, but also as a means of driving thieves to the deadliest locations.

### 3. Blossom Averye

Best friend of Willow Averille (page 107) and co-founding member of the famed Coalition for Magical Cooperation (CMC), a loose partnership of high level Magic-Users and Clerics intent on doing good. But it seems that the mandate may have to grow beyond what it originally was. Her sources say that four invaders—The Tetrad—have been linked to many disappearances and deaths. The CMC may not have the strength or the numbers to fight them. It would not be the first time that Blossom had to meet a challenge. It’s been a necessity since her youth. Blossom is an only child and regards Willow as the sister she never had. For a while, she felt like she could only watch helplessly as Willow hurtled into despair. It looked like rage and megalomania to a lot of people, but Blossom knew where it had come from and what it meant. Remaining as the only listening ear while so many abandoned Willow was the most dificult thing she’d ever done. At times it felt like Willow screamed just to force people out of her life. She felt so empty and worthless, anger became part of fulfilling a destiny that was inevitable. She deserved no one, everyone would leave her eventually anyway— why not just get it over with quicker? But Blossom wouldn’t leave. She didn’t know then whether Willow felt the sense of sisterhood that she did. Probably not. Willow didn’t understand family. But allowing Willow to disappear into herself was something Blossom wouldn’t allow. So, she weathered the torrent, waited for the clearing, and pulled her friend back to shore. It wasn’t quick; the current against them was strong. But in the end they not only saved a friendship, but became like family, and later formed “the famed Coalition for Magical Cooperation.” ‘Til death do them part.

### 4. Leofsige Alvine

Elder cousin of Alvina (page 31) he has all of the maturity she currently lacks, but was always a radically diferent personality. He reads voraciously, networks extensively, and ponders deeply. Face to face with him you discover someone that is extremely laid back and carefully measured. He eases into things slowly before fully committing and isn’t given to changing his mind without a compelling reason. He is idealistic, loyal, and a firm adherent of law and morality. To that end, he’s become greatly concerned by a number of people he’s come to refer to as The Tetrad. They seemingly have nothing in common except their utter disdain for others, and the number of injuries, deaths and disappearances that coincide with their proximity. They seem to have no clear motive or discernible plan. Leofsige brought his concerns to the one person he knew would understand the gravity as well as himself, Mallory Freemane (page 92). Together they began secretly assembling a surveillance force, The Octad, to gather intelligence in preparation for a response. While he often functions in the role of oficial Lead, at its heart The Octad is a partnership. The members comprising five of the six remaining places were selected by Mallory based on her own contacts. The final slot, occupied by Themistocles Spinther (page 85), was at Leofsige’s invitation because of their long and trusting friendship.

### 5. Vidar Ebbe

His names combined mean “strong boar,” but because his interests were arcane, not athletic, he was occasionally teased as the “strong bore.” A smack upside the head from his dexterous hand generally convinced people that he wasn’t all just book-smarts. This is especially true after successfully dodging the blows that sometimes came in return. Because of those days, he doesn’t enjoy working with “big dumb fighters” but he’s no snob. If he feels respected and appreciated he’s happy to throw his lot in with a group of adventurers. If he just could find a decent bunch, he’d really prefer just to settle down and stop looking. He’s worth talking to. He throws knives like that wizard at the fair did last year. You know the one. Everyone has heard of him, even though they don’t always recall his name.

### 6. Odovacar Alcimus Libo

An influential public personality, he—perhaps more than any other living Magic-User—has brought more new hopefuls into the discipline than any wizard before or since. Long past his dungeon crawling days, his public presentations are a calculated and thrilling combination of violently spectacular spellcasting and thrown steel. We’ll never know how many Magic-Users began collecting discarded daggers in a sack and learning the art of throwing after having watched Odovacar pierce strawmen with deadly accuracy, before burning them to the ground with a flick of a pinky finger. You’re imagining it right now, aren’t you? Far from a hack showman, he’s reticent to calculate (and would just as soon forget) the number of living monsters that died horribly as he honed his skills in combat. On his personal time, he memorizes a diferent set of spells—ones useful in protecting himself from obsessed fans, and enemies. In the event he is threatened and outnumbered, he typically kills the strongest attacker he sees in gruesome fashion, then animates the body to turn on its former party members. This is quite efective with the intense psychological terror it induces. Upon ceasing control, he’ll then leave the body as a warning to would-be criminals of the dire consequences of tangling with a wizard. Definitely not a charlatan. Whatever the crowd, whatever the circumstances, he knows exactly how to create the desired efect in his audience.

### 7. Carling Truemane

In private, Carling laughs at his name. It suggests that he is what he seems to be, whereas he’s actually a Cloud Dragon (Age Category 6). It’s an interesting time to watch Humans. They’re closer now than they’ve ever been to extinction, and he’s got a front row seat. He has knowledge of more spells than he should for a wizard of this level, but in the interest in staying undercover he’s dutifully keeping a modest selection (at the GM’s option, he could have the full number his age permits). His treasure is far away and he’s got nothing to lose except his seat. If he is somehow found out, he will use whatever defensive means he can to create a distraction, then teleport away. He’ll then appear in a diferent region with a new name and a new appearance, but the same desire to witness what might be the end of the world, as it happens. For now though, things are splendid. He’s even opening his mind to exploring a romantic relationship with a Human female, Zanna Chadwicke (page 98), as a cover, but also as an experiment he’s yet to attempt over his long years.

### 8. Gautbert Gravese

Gautbert’s specialty is the acquisition and training of special creatures to become familiars. He is most certainly not interested in going on any adventures; his inventory of creatures cannot be left unsupervised. However, he may be able to provide spell tutoring for Magic-Users and provide magical research equipment and materials for cost plus a nominal service fee. At any given time, there is a 50% chance that his training facility in the dungeon of his tower may contain 1 brownie, 1 (Infernal) imp, 1 pseudodragon, and 1 (Infernal) quasit (roll for each creature type). He does not ofer cats, crows, dogs, hawks, owls, ravens, toads, or weasels. For those, talk to Godabert Grieves (page 72). Yes, people do get them mixed up all the time. They’re used to it. And no that is not a hawk behind him, It’s a very naughty brownie trying to get your attention. And that raven is an imp who’s going to be in very big trouble unless she stops shape-changing right this instant.
