### 1. Clayton Turnbulle

Clayton desperately aspires to become a leader but doesn’t understand what that entails. He has a certain charisma and intellect and his physical prowess is significant, but he hasn't found a way to put it all together into a package that commands attention. He’s not a gifted speech- maker nor a tactical genius nor an organizational visionary. He can briefly inspire interest, but his lack of ideas and poor social skills quickly dissipates it. He dreams of scores of underlings, following with blind loyalty. Those around him wonder how it is that he's so enamoured with himself.

Conrad isn’t exactly old, but you wouldn’t know it by the way he constantly vents about aches, pains, and the intimate details of his bodily functions. ### 3. Nicomedes Varus

Nicomedes found himself vaulted into a leadership role due to his talent for brokering peace between the other contenders for the role. No one enjoyed the squabbling, but no one wanted to be the one to show weakness and acquiesce either. Nicomedes stepped into that gap and won hearts and minds as the reasonable alternative between warring factions. It’s an illusion. He’s no less power hungry, but does have a talent for reading a room, understanding how a course of events will unfold, biding his time, choosing his words very carefully, and delivering his message in a way that inspires calm confidence. Once he’s afirmed in a formal position of power, his ruthlessness will be allowed out to play. He is unforgiving of those who might cross him, and with the contest already won he will not waste time with a measured response.

### 4. Tuesday Rey

Senior member of a covert group of overseers, The Octad. She was Mallory Freemane’s (page 92) first selection for candidates to add to the then-fledgling organization she co-founded with Leofsige Alvine (page 114). Never a killer, Tuesday was a robber of dungeons and catacombs—and perhaps incidentally, one or two monstrous Humans—before switching to the information trade as she grew more experienced. Extroverted and intuitive, she excels at getting people to talk about things they later can’t believe they said. She’s also excellent at getting into places where she’s likely to overhear valuable information. In her early days this was mostly through skilled climbing and disguise kits. Now she can pretty much talk herself into any room.

### 5. Embla Jorunn

A horse lover since she was a young girl, it was always a dream of hers to have a big, beautiful farmhouse under the shelter of plenty of shady elm trees, and stable full of beautiful thoroughbreds out back. She didn’t put a lot of thought into how much this dream might cost, but in the end she didn’t need to. You paid for it; or if not you, people like you. It all started at a tavern. She saw a group of adventurers sitting around a table planning their next sojourn, and at the end of the night she followed them home. Two had rooms upstairs at the tavern’s inn; two were townies whom she already recognized. It was a dificult night, but in the end those men had no other choice but to go adventuring—she’d left them with nothing but the coins in the pockets of the clothes they slept in. She realized she had a business model. She has to be careful not to be seen in the same taverns too often, and she lets a few parties go here and there just to allay suspicion, but it keeps the horses fed.

### 6. Corliss Dunne

Intelligent and wise but world-weary, Corliss is demonstrating wear and tear from her (alternately, his) scrapes with earthly creatures over the years. Her once care-free, live-and-let-live approach has nearly evaporated. Measured words have given way to jarring pronouncements; polite white lies discarded for bitter truths. She’s aware that she’s a drastically diferent Halfling now than the adventurous, patient, goodhumoured one that once was. She does her best to do a good impression of that person when meeting old friends. But for strangers and new acquaintances, she just couldn’t be bothered. Anyone expecting the warmth and sociable charm her race is famous for will be sorely disappointed —and she doesn’t care. She attributes the change to too much time spent around Humans. It’s not a bad thing. It’s just reality. Choices have consequences, and she is the product of her experiences.

### 7. Harper Durand

Friendly-seeming but greedy and conscienceless, Harper was a natural fit for estate auctions and property sales. If only he was the one authorized to settle the afairs of the departed. A lot of his work is done through finders. He pays dozens of low-level thieves to travel and case the region for prospects, staying at inns at his expense. The elderly and ill are first picks, but the middle-aged and isolated or estranged are also often promising. Surveillance determines whether the property and contents warrant further action. Should the prospect linger too long, a moderate dose of poison generally speeds things to a conclusion. It is then that Harper makes his appearance to handle the disposition with his practised blend of authority and sensitivity. 

Dwarf Thief 9, AC 17, AB +5, #At 1, Dam 1d8+1, Mv 40', ML 9, 

STR 10, INT 12, WIS 16 (+2), 

DEX 16 (+2), CON 12, CHA 15 (+1) 

Equipment: Leather Armour +2, Longsword +1, map to treasure Type H 

HP 31 ☐☐☐☐☐ ☐☐☐☐☐ ☐☐☐☐☐ ☐☐☐☐☐ ☐☐☐☐☐ ☐☐☐☐☐ ☐ 

### 8. Chrysogon Mnemosyne

I questioned whether or not I should write an entry for myself. It seems egotistical, and yet I must acknowledge that I am a person of interest —if only as the author of all these glimpses into the memories and motivations of others. When I die—or if I abruptly leave this place and start over somewhere else, as I’ve so often done before—I know this book will be discovered and read. So, I will record something of my life here as an indulgence of your curiosity. Just know that this book was never intended for you. It was written for me. 

Many of the names in this book have meanings, and where I thought it was appropriate, I noted how the person’s destiny either upheld or disgraced the one they had been given. My name means “Gold Born” and in my childhood I wanted for nothing. My father was a ruler. My mother was the daughter of another. While it is often the custom that sons are married to daughters to create political alliances and concentrate wealth and power—and my parents were no exception— they grew to love each other in the way we all wish to become true for our own unions. 

They lavished attention, afection, praise, culture, education, and opportunity upon me, and one would assume that I grew up happy and appreciative of my good fortune. I did not. With no basis of comparison, my life felt perfectly ordinary to me. There were no obstacles, disappointments, or heartbreaks; no epiphanies, achievements, or transformational experiences. I just was, and the emptiness was intolerable to me. 

With my father’s position, I had an inordinate level of freedom which I exploited fully. I stole, I lied, then stole even more. I spread rumours of others’ culpability then planted evidence in support of it. I looked for people with enviable success and strong relationships and used all the skills I possessed to leave them humiliated, isolated and broken to the point where some chose not to live anymore. 

When I feared that I was about to be discovered I bade my parents goodbye, but not before taking the inheritance I believed would have been mine eventually. I would never return. I discarded my name and lived under dozens of others. I rose swiftly through the many guilds that were my home, rising to the top only to become bored and needing to begin anew in another city. I had no interest in leadership. Watching the chaos incited by my abrupt exit from a thriving enterprise was incalculably satisfying—creating dependence and then causing a void. An implosion of greed, entitlement, betrayal and violence. Beautiful madness. 

![image](https://cdn-mineru.openxlab.org.cn/result/2026-09-15/807cc405-0084-4a45-8e24-5c7f50c4cc53/316f53047aa157bc711f1122d416377984a24d67429f8750a1a4e078d18f6fc7.jpg)


It took many repetitions of this behaviour to realize that the void that had always existed inside of me was infinite. Nothing filled it, and I’d learned instead to rejoice in spreading emptiness to others. It took longer still for me to understand that nothing about this path in life had done anything more than keep me a step ahead from being consumed by the emptiness. 

And then I quit. And I could, because I had somewhere to hide from it all. For all my wandering, I’d at one point realized I needed a place for my treasure and I bought a castle—not a guild. Naturally I couldn’t trust thieves to manage my afairs in my absence, but a number of men who fully understood my capabilities signed on as my hired staf. Having solved serious problems for each of them, they were loyal through a mix of fear and awe. When I returned unexpectedly I found that all was well, and they’ve served me faithfully to this day. 

I live under the pseudonym I created solely for the ownership of this castle and I am never seen. Everything I need is brought to me, and anything I send is delivered by trusted hands. I am content to be known to the common folk as a wealthy and possibly insane eccentric. It keeps them from scaling the walls. 

This book that you hold was not written for you, but for me. It has allowed me to escape myself, but to do so alone. While I never was able to feel, I quickly mastered the capacity to recognize feelings in others. While I never was able to truly befriend anyone, I was always a very good listener. And while I never felt I could belong anywhere, my indelible memory allowed me to retain the names and details of those whose paths I crossed, which are now catalogued here. 

Time spent distracting myself recording these lives has made mine more tolerable. Seeing these words, plainly staring back at me from the page, has made them more consequential than was the case when they were bits of unconnected trivia filed in my head. Reflecting on the work in its totality has given me much to think about. 

It is not a surprise to me to see pages upon pages of individuals who chose to squander their lives as foolishly as I did. More striking are the numbers who chose to do good, despite the existence of the rest of us, with no expectation of glory or reward, but just doggedly doing good for good’s sake. 

I can’t understand why.

And now that I’ve indulged you, this exercise is complete. I don’t know how it is that you’ve come to read this, but it is not my legacy or a gift to living-kind. 

If I’m to be remembered at all, understand that the greatest kindness I showed the world was not to contribute, but to withdraw.
